Higher-order quantum processes respecting closed labs in a spacetime have quantum controlled causal order
Matthias Salzger, V. Vilasini

Abstract
In quantum causality and quantum information, there is a vast landscape of abstract quantum protocols that permit cyclic or non-acyclic causal structures between quantum operations. This includes widely studied frameworks for indefinite causal order and higher-order quantum processes, such as process matrices. However, a longstanding open question has been which is the largest class of such abstract processes that admit physical realisations without post-selection. In this work, we provide a rigorous answer by adopting a top-down approach grounded in relativistic causality principles, motivated by the fact that physical experiments are implemented consistently with such principles in spacetimes with acyclic lightcone structures. Building on the framework of causal boxes, which characterise the most general quantum information-processing protocols compatible with fixed background spacetimes, we formalise additional physically motivated constraints (Acting Once + Local Order) capturing the closed-laboratory assumptions of the process matrix framework at a fine-grained spacetime level. We prove that any protocol realisable in a classical acyclic spacetime and satisfying these spatiotemporal closed-lab conditions is behaviourally equivalent to a quantum circuit with quantum control of causal orders (QC-QC), providing a top-down derivation of QC-QCs from physical principles. Our results therefore show that QC-QCs constitute precisely the class of higher-order quantum processes, including those with indefinite orders, that can be physically realised within classical spacetime, clearly ruling out the possibility of any experiment in this regime that could realise more general non-causal processes under such a closed-labs assumption. This clarifies the relationship between abstract higher-order process matrix frameworks and experimentally accessible quantum protocols, as well as the interplay between coarse-grained cyclic and fine-grained acyclic operational causal structures. We also develop characterisation techniques and results for process box protocols that lead to new causality-based open questions concerning spacetime quantum protocols and relativistic quantum experiments.



1
Introduction

Many frameworks used in quantum information theory are formulated without explicit reference to any underlying spacetime. This abstraction has led to a broad landscape of general quantum protocols, where the (information-theoretic) causal structure between quantum operations need not be definite and acyclic, allowing for intriguing situations involving paradox-free quantum causal loops and protocols with indefinite causal order (ICO) of agents’ quantum operations. The most general such abstract and spacetime agnostic quantum protocols that can be formulated in a compositional manner arise by considering sets of quantum operations that are composed in a well-defined manner, but possibly through feedback loops. These possibly cyclic structures can equivalently be described in several frameworks such as postselected closed timelike curves (P-CTCs)
[
43
]
, category-theoretic approaches to quantum theory
[
54
,
64
,
20
]
, cyclic quantum networks
[
74
,
75
]
, and the cyclic quantum causal modelling formalism of
[
27
]
1
1

1

A prior formalism for cyclic quantum causal models
[
6
]
also exists, but this models a strict subset of P-CTCs which correspond to valid process operators.
.

However, current day quantum experiments are implemented within the regime of a classical background spacetime, whose light cone structure is acyclic. This raises the question of the connection between abstract quantum protocols and actual experiments and, once this has been clarified, which of these quantum protocols can then be realised within such experiments without invoking post-selection (as it is known that all such general cyclic protocols can be realised experimentally if we allow post-selection). A first step toward answering this from a top-down perspective was taken in
[
74
]
, where it was shown that the most general quantum protocols (including those with cycles such as P-CTCs) that can be realised in a background spacetime compatible with relativistic causality, correspond to so-called causal boxes
[
56
]
. Causal boxes model quantum protocols on an explicit background spacetime and allow for physical feedback loops consistent with relativistic causality (as opposed to causal loops permitting retrocausality), multiple rounds of information processing, superpositions in the spacetime position of quantum systems and are fully closed under composition. However, this leaves open the precise relationship between the aforementioned abstract protocols and protocols realisable within spacetime.

Within the spacetime agnostic landscape, a broad set of abstract quantum protocols that has received considerable interest goes variously under the names indefinite causal order, higher-order processes or process matrices
[
37
,
48
,
Chiribella2013
]
. These frameworks model quantum protocols where agents perform arbitrary quantum operations within “closed labs”, each agent receiving an input quantum system from and releasing an output quantum system to an “environment” exactly once, and they allow for protocols that lack a definite acyclic causal order between the quantum operations performed by the different agents.
Such process matrices are known to be a linear subset of the aforementioned P-CTCs
[
5
]
. Various works have studied them for possible applications and advantages compared to protocols with a more standard fixed causal order
[
19
,
21
,
3
,
liu2024
,
34
,
16
,
82
,
35
,
25
,
66
]
. In particular, some indefinite causal order processes are said to be causally non-separable, meaning that they cannot be decomposed as convex mixtures of processes with a definite (but possibly dynamic) acyclic causal order
[
48
,
2
,
49
]
. This can be thought of as analogous to entangled or non-separable quantum states, which cannot be expressed as convex mixtures of product states. Other process matrices can violate so-called causal inequalities which yield device-independent certification of the absence of such definite acyclic causal order, which can be thought of as analogous to Bell inequalities
[
48
,
10
]
. Several other interesting subsets and examples of process matrices (or equivalently, quantum super maps or higher-order quantum processes) have been identified and continue to be widely studied (see e.g.,
[
9
,
33
,
76
,
78
,
62
,
47
,
67
]
).

However, a longstanding open question is the following:
Which indefinite causal order processes can be physically realised?
This question has been approached by imposing information-theoretic principles such as purifiability to narrow down the space of possible ICO processes
[
4
,
18
]
, but this still permits processes such as the Lugano process
[
8
]
which violate causal inequalities, where no clear physical interpretation (within known physics) has been found.
2
2

2

Although explicit models of this process within several operationally motivated frameworks such as routed quantum circuits and time delocalised subsystems have been proposed
[
72
,
77
]
.

The central question considered in this work adds a relativistic refinement to the above, in the spirit of
[
74
]
while considering the physical regime of currently performable quantum experiments:
Which indefinite causal order processes can be physically realised through experiments (in principle), in a meaningful way, in a classical acyclic spacetime?
Answering this question involves clarifying what “meaningful” entails, as generic experiments may be incompatible with the setup assumptions of the process matrix framework such as “closed labs” needed to ensure that certifications of indefinite order developed in that framework remain reliable. The situation is analogous to Bell scenarios and experiments. If an experiment produces a Bell violation, this is insufficient to call this a genuine violation of a Bell inequality or regard this as a genuinely non-classical phenomenon, even if the correlations produced are non-signalling. Instead, there are certain additional conditions which must be satisfied in the experimental setup (e.g., that the agents are unable to communicate due to spacelike separation) in order to ensure that the observed Bell inequality violation can be regarded as “loophole free”. Only under such constraints do we consider the Bell inequality violation to be meaningful
[
38
,
30
,
65
]
.

Understanding the physical structure of process matrices has also been approached in a bottom-up manner in
[
78
]
by taking the notion of a quantum circuit and generalising it to allow for indefinite causal order, but without explicit reference to spacetime. The resulting quantum circuits with quantum control of causal orders (QC-QCs) exhibit quantum superpositions of the order of the agents’ operations which can be controlled by some quantum system or dynamically by the choice of other agents’ operations. In a previous work
[
61
]
, we have shown that all QC-QCs can be embedded into classical spacetime as causal boxes satisfying a spatiotemporal version of the closed laboratory assumption of process matrices. We also showed there, in a constructive manner, that all QC-QCs, even those with indefinite causal order, once realised in spacetime as above, admit a description in terms of a definite acyclic causal order between more fine-grained operations, consistent with relativistic causality (using the notion of fine-graining defined in
[
74
]
). This makes precise in what sense QC-QCs can be thought of as physical protocols in classical spacetime but leaves the question of whether they are the most general process matrices that could be physically realised in this regime, which is the question we address here.

Finally, it is important to mention that several sophisticated table-top experiments
[
57
,
59
,
31
,
80
,
60
,
36
,
32
,
70
]
have already claimed to physically implement a canonical example of a QC-QC having ICO, the
quantum switch

[
Chiribella2013
]
. The physical interpretation of such experiments, and whether they have succeeded in genuinely implementing indefinite causal order in Minkowski spacetime has been a subject of intense debate, that has touched upon deep foundational questions on the very definition of events and their localisation in quantum theory
[
74
,
Chiribella2013
,
53
,
23
,
42
,
50
,
51
,
26
,
73
]
. We will not go into the details of this debate here, but we will briefly return to it in
Section

8
to clarify how our results and conclusions hold independently of the “side” that one takes on this debate.

Summary of contributions and structure of paper:

The main contribution of this work is to show that any protocol or experiment realisable in an acyclic background spacetime that respects the closed labs assumption of the process matrix framework, necessarily behaves as a QC-QC. This answers the longstanding question on physical realisability of process matrices within this classical spacetime regime and provides a top-down derivation of the structure of QC-QCs from physical principles. Our result includes a large class of spacetimes allowed by general relativity and covers the regime of quantum experiments to date, in particular Minkowski spacetime as well as curved, globally hyperbolic spacetimes, but treated as a classical background that does not share back-reaction with the quantum process being realised.

In achieving this main result, we introduce a number of new techniques, additional results and examples which also outline future directions that might be of independent interest (see also
Fig.

1
for a visual summary of some of these). We review the causal box framework in
Section

2.1
and the higher-order processes and QC-QC frameworks in
Section

2.2
. A detailed summary of all main contributions along with the structure of the rest of the paper are given below.

•

The framework (causal box and process box protocols):
In
Section

3
, we first introduce multi-partite causal box (CB) protocols, for describing relativistic quantum information protocols involving
N
agents interacting with each other via an “environment”, where the agents’ operations and the environment are both modelled as causal boxes. This brings CBs one step closer to the set-up of process matrices (PMs). However, observing that such CB protocols still allow for trivial, maximal violations of causal inequalities (
Example

1
) that is not possible with PMs, in
Section

4.1
, we restrict them further via two key principles capturing the closed labs assumption of the PM framework at the fine-grained, spacetime level: Acting Once (AO) and Local Order (LO), together called
spatiotemporal closed labs
, which define the subset of CB protocols called process box (PB) protocols. Originally the idea of restricting CBs through such conditions, to define PBs was proposed in
[
Vilasini_2020
]
. Here we fully formalise this at the level of CB and PB protocols, showing that these are joint properties of the “environment” and the agent operations, and thus providing a more general formalisation of AO and LO than in
[
Vilasini_2020
]
, with the interest of allowing the most general possible spacetime scenarios that can be said to respect the set-up assumptions of the process matrix framework. Moreover, we have provided an alternative formalisation of these conditions in a compositional manner, allowing easier characterisation and connection to other frameworks.

•

Characterisation results for process boxes:
Even with the spatiotemporal closed labs assumption imposed, general PB protocols can look very different from known process matrices or relevant subsets thereof. In
Section

4.2
, we detail these differences, and then present a key characterisation result (
1
) which shows that the structure of PB protocols can in fact be considerably simplified while preserving their relevant operational behaviour. In particular, this includes simplifying the partially ordered spacetime to a totally ordered one, which uses an important property of generalised invariance under relabelling (of spacetime locations) of CB protocols established in
Lemma

5
and from time-dependent to time-independent agent operations in PB protocols.

•

Mapping process boxes to QC-QCs:
In
Section

5
, we first present an example of a physically motivated PB protocol, based on the experiment for a long coherence time quantum switch
[
31
,
32
]
, and its mapping to QC-QCs, highlighting that multiple PB protocols (more fine-grained description) can map to the same QC-QC protocol (coarse-grained description), just as an abstract process can have many different physical realisations. We then proceed to give our general mapping from process boxes to QC-QCs, defining what it means for them to be
behaviourally equivalent
, and culminating in the main theorem (
2
) which shows that for every PB protocol, there exists a behaviourally equivalent QC-QC protocol.

•

Physical principles and ruling out the Lugano process:
An important question in quantum causality has been to understand why the so-called Lugano process, a highly studied ICO process violating causal inequalities, can or cannot be physically realised. Previous work
[
77
]
showed that the Lugano process admits a realisation in the framework of time-delocalised subsystems
[
50
]
. Our results, on the other hand, provide a top-down approach from physical principles, to explain why it is impossible to realise the Lugano process in any experiment in a fixed spacetime which respects the closed labs assumption even on a fine-grained level. We compare our results and assumptions with those of
[
77
]
in detail, to illustrate how there is no mathematical contradiction between the two results. In essence, by virtue of being a regular quantum circuit, the Lugano process construction of
[
77
]
can be described in terms of valid causal boxes
[
56
]
and thus respects the general definitions of spatiotemporal realisation and relativistic causality proposed in
[
74
,
75
]
.
3
3

3

[
74
,
75
]
only defined necessary conditions for spatiotemporal realisations of processes and relativistic causality, sufficient for the general no-go theorems considered there. Those conditions are insufficient to guarantee that such a “realisation” is loophole free i.e., respects the set-up assumptions like closed labs, which are further formalised at the fine-grained level, in the process box framework considered here.
However, it is not a valid process box protocol as it violates the spatiotemporal closed labs condition of this framework. There is no contradiction because
the closed labs condition of
[
77
]
is strictly weaker than our spatiotemporal closed labs, such that the Lugano process satisfies the former but necessarily violates the latter. We argue that for loophole free tests of causal inequalities (analogous to such tests of Bell inequalities), one must impose such assumptions at the spacetime implementation level as we do (as opposed to the abstract level), and the analysis highlights that realisation with time delocalised subsystems (although interesting in its own right) is not sufficient to ensure physical and loophole free experimental realisations in a background spacetime.

•

Possible relaxations of the assumptions:
In
Section

7
, we detail the scope for further relaxing our spatiotemporal closed labs (i.e., AO+LO) assumption, distinguishing weak vs strong form of violation of each of AO and LO. While our example (
Example

1
) for trivial violation of causal inequalities, as well as the Lugano process (
Example

6
) violate at least one of these conditions strongly (and cannot be mapped to QC-QCs), we present a novel example in
Example

7
which violates both of them weakly and can still be mapped to a behaviourally equivalent QC-QC protocol. This outlines clear scope for further generalising our results, as well as a number of open questions on the characterisation of causal box protocols, and their relationships (in particular, via coarse-graining) with different classes of abstract cyclic quantum causal structures (see also
Fig.

1
).

2
Review

2.1
Causal boxes

2.1.1
Messages and Fock spaces

The causal box framework models a background spacetime minimally as a partially ordered set
\mathcal{T}
as a partial order is sufficient to capture the lightcone structure, which is the main object of interest for causality. In the original work
[
56
]
, the set
\mathcal{T}
can be infinite, whereas for us the finite case will be sufficient.

A quantum message
\ket{\psi}
sent/received at time
t
is represented as a state
\ket{\psi}^{A}\otimes\ket{t}^{\mathcal{T}}
. we will usually write this as
\ket{\psi,t}^{A,\mathcal{T}}
or
\ket{\psi,t}^{A}
for compactness, dropping the superscript
\mathcal{T}
if the set of positions is unambiguous. The element
\ket{\psi}^{A}\in\mathcal{H}^{A}
encodes the information of the message sent or received on wire
A
while
\ket{t}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{T}}\cong\mathbb{C}^{|\mathcal{T}|}
tells us the position of the message in spacetime.

The space
\mathcal{H}^{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{T}}
, which we will usually write compactly as
\mathcal{H}^{A,\mathcal{T}}
is then the 1-message space. This is, however, not the full state space. More generally, the framework allows an arbitrary number of messages distributed over spacetime, including superpositions of different number of such messages, which can be modelled using the symmetric Fock space associated with this 1-message space

\mathcal{F}^{A,\mathcal{T}}:=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{H}^{A,\mathcal{T}})=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\vee^{n}\mathcal{H}^{A,\mathcal{T}}.

(1)

The symbol
\vee^{n}
denotes
n
-fold symmetric tensor product. The symmetric Fock space is used here as the position labels already include all the ordering relations. This is particularly important for messages at the same position, as the two states
\ket{0,t}^{A}\otimes\ket{1,t}^{A}
and
\ket{1,t}^{A}\otimes\ket{0,t}^{A}
should really be undistinguishable. The one-dimensional subspace
(\mathcal{H}^{A,\mathcal{T}})^{\otimes 0}
of the Fock space
\mathcal{F}^{A,\mathcal{T}}
corresponds to the vacuum state
\ket{\Omega}^{A,\mathcal{T}}\cong\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}^{A}
,
i.e., there are no messages on the wire
A
at any position in
\mathcal{T}
.

There are two wire isomorphisms in the causal box framework
[
56
]
which we will use frequently and implicitly (that is to say, we will use equality signs, even if the equality only holds up to these isomorphisms) in this work. For any
\mathcal{H}^{A},\mathcal{H}^{B}
and any
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{F}^{C,\mathcal{T}}&\cong\mathcal{F}^{A,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{B,\mathcal{T}}\\
\mathcal{F}^{A,\mathcal{T}}&\cong\mathcal{F}^{A,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{A,\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\end{aligned}

(2)

where
\mathcal{H}^{C}=\mathcal{H}^{A}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{B}
. These isomorphisms tell us that we can split and combine wires. Two wires carrying messages corresponding to the Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}^{A},\mathcal{H}^{B}
can be combined into a single wire carrying messages corresponding to the direct sum. Similarly, two wires corresponding to two different spacetime regions can be combined into a single wire covering both regions. As we will see, these isomorphisms allow to first define causal boxes with just a single input and output wire which can then be split up into multiple in and outputs later when it is convenient.

We can also naturally embed any wire corresponding to some spacetime region
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{F}^{A,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
, into a larger spacetime region
\mathcal{T}
, by appending the vacuum on
\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.

\mathcal{F}^{A,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\cong\mathcal{F}^{A,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}.

(3)

2.1.2
Definition of causal boxes

We give now a simplified definition of causal boxes that captures those causal boxes defined on a finite
\mathcal{T}
. This captures that causal boxes respect special relativity, meaning that there should be no signalling outside the lightcone (given by the partial order of
\mathcal{T}
). This is formalised with the help of causality functions.

Definition 1

(Causality function
[
56
]
)
.

A causality function
\chi
for a finite spacetime
\mathcal{T}
maps bottom-closed subsets
4
4

4

A set
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
is bottom-closed (in
\mathcal{T}
) iff for all
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
with
t\preceq t^{\prime}
,
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
implies
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. In
[
56
]
, the more involved notions of cuts and bounded cuts (Def. 4.2 there) of the spacetime is used. However, in our case (i.e., finite spacetimes) there is no distinction between bottom-closed subsets, cuts and bounded cuts.
of
\mathcal{T}
to bottom-closed subsets of
\mathcal{T}
and satisfies the following conditions for any non-empty
\mathcal{T}^{\prime},\mathcal{T}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
which are bottom-closed

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\prime\prime})&=\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\cup\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime\prime})\\
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{\prime\prime}&\implies\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\subseteq\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime\prime})\\
\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})&\subsetneq\mathcal{T}^{\prime}.\end{aligned}

(4)

The causality function tells us that in order to compute the outputs in some bottom-closed spacetime region
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, it is sufficient to know the inputs in a strict bottom-closed subset of that region given by
\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
. The fact that it is proper subset and that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is bottom-closed (i.e., it includes the past lightcones of all its elements) ensures consistency with special relativity.

Definition 2

(Causal boxes
[
56
]
)
.

A causal box is a system with an input wire
X
and an output wire
Y
, together with a CPTP map

\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}})

(5)

that fulfils for all
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
which are bottom-closed

\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C}=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})}

(6)

for some causality function
\chi
, where
\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
(
\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})}
) corresponds to tracing out all messages with positions in
\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
(
\mathcal{T}\backslash\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
) and replacing it with the vacuum (this can also be understood in light of
Eq.

3
). We call
Eq.

6
the causality condition.

Note that from this definition it directly follows that a causal box restricted to some set of positions
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
which is bottom-closed is once again a causal box.

We can also consider subnormalised causal boxes. For this to be well-defined it is not sufficient to just replace the CPTP map in
2
with a CP map but to explicitly define subnormalised causal boxes as particular outcomes of causal boxes.

Definition 3

(Subnormalised causal boxes)
.

We call
\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}})
a subnormalised causal box if there exists a (normalised) causal box
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{YR,\mathcal{T}})
and a product state
\ket{\psi}^{R}=\bigotimes_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\ket{\psi_{t},t}
such that

\mathcal{C}(\cdot)=\bra{\psi}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\cdot)\ket{\psi}.

(7)

Operationally, subnormalised causal boxes thus correspond to post-selecting the state on wire
R
. We note that in
[
56
]
, the state
\ket{\psi}^{R}
is assumed to always be the vacuum state. The two definitions are equivalent, but the above definition makes it easier to model different measurement outcomes as different subnormalised causal boxes obtained from the same normalised causal box, which will be useful for us later on.

As the vacuum state is a product state it is clear that our definition here is at least as general as the definition of
[
56
]
. On the other hand, if we have
\mathcal{C}(\cdot)=\bra{\psi}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\cdot)\ket{\psi}
as in the definition above with
\ket{\psi}^{R}=\bigotimes_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\ket{\psi_{t},t}
, then there exists for each
t\in\mathcal{T}
a unitary
U_{t}:\mathcal{F}^{R,t}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{R,t}
such that
U_{t}\ket{\psi_{t},t}=\ket{\Omega,t}
. From this it follows immediately that
\bra{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}\bigotimes_{t\in\mathcal{T}}U_{t}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\cdot)\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}}U_{t^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}=\bigotimes_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\bra{\psi,t}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\cdot)\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}}\ket{\psi_{t^{\prime}},t^{\prime}}=\mathcal{C}(\cdot)
. The map
\bigotimes_{t\in\mathcal{T}}U_{t}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\cdot)\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}}U_{t^{\prime}}
is a causal box as it is evidently CPTP and
\text{tr}_{t}U_{t}\cdot U_{t}^{\dagger}=\text{tr}_{t}(\cdot)
due to unitarity of
U_{t}
. Indeed, this argument shows that for any product state
\ket{\psi}
there exists a normalised causal box
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
such that
Eq.

7
holds.

2.1.3
Representations of causal boxes

There are two particularly useful representations of causal boxes, one of these being the Choi-Jamiołkowski representation and the other the so-called sequence representation
[
56
]
. The Choi-Jamiołkowski representation, however, involves some subtleties as the normal Choi-Jamiołkowski operator may become unbounded and thus ill-defined when considering infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces
[
41
]
. In this case, the Choi-Jamiołkowski representation of a CPTP map
\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{X})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{Y})
is a sesquilinear form defined on
\mathcal{H}^{X}\times\mathcal{H}^{Y}=\text{span}\{\ket{\psi^{X}}\otimes\ket{\psi^{Y}}|\ket{\psi^{X}}\in\mathcal{H}^{X},\ket{\psi^{Y}}\in\mathcal{H}^{Y}\}
(i.e., the
finite
linear span) as follows

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R_{\mathcal{C}}&:(\mathcal{H}^{X}\times\mathcal{H}^{Y})\times(\mathcal{H}^{X}\times\mathcal{H}^{Y})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\\
&\ket{\psi^{X}}\otimes\ket{\psi^{Y}}\otimes\ket{\varphi^{X}}\otimes\ket{\varphi^{Y}}\mapsto\bra{\psi^{Y}}\mathcal{C}(\ket{\bar{\psi}^{X}}\bra{\bar{\varphi}^{X}})\ket{\varphi^{Y}}\end{aligned}

(8)

where
\ket{\bar{\psi}^{X}}=\sum_{i}\ket{i}\overline{\braket{i|\psi^{X}}}
. This is more compactly written as

R_{\mathcal{C}}(\psi^{X}\otimes\psi^{Y},\varphi^{X}\otimes\varphi^{Y}):=\bra{\psi^{Y}}\mathcal{C}(\ket{\bar{\psi}^{X}}\bra{\bar{\varphi}^{X}})\ket{\varphi^{Y}}

(9)

If
\mathcal{H}^{X}\times\mathcal{H}^{Y}
coincides with
\mathcal{H}^{X}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{Y}
, then the usual Choi-Jamiołkowski operator is bounded and can be recovered from this sesquilinear form
[
41
,
56
]
.

The sequence representation is a sequence of isometries
V_{1},...,V_{M}
, such that
V=V_{M}\circ V_{M-1}\circ...\circ V_{1}
is a purification of the causal box. Further, each
V_{m}
acts exclusively on some disjoint slice of spacetime
\mathcal{T}_{m}
and outputs on the “next” slice
\mathcal{T}_{m+1}
, the idea thus being that
V_{m}
represents the action of
\mathcal{C}
on this particular slice of the overall spacetime
\mathcal{T}
. More explicitly, for a causal box with input wire
X
, output wire
Y
,
V_{1}
prepares a pure normalised state on
\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}}
,
V_{m}:\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}_{m-1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{m-1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}_{m}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{m}}
for
1<m<M
and
V_{M}:\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}_{M-1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{M-1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{M}}
. The sets
\{\mathcal{T}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{M-1}
form a partition of
\mathcal{T}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{T}=\bigsqcup_{m=1}^{M-1}\mathcal{T}_{m}
(where
\bigsqcup
denotes disjoint union) and are ordered in the sense that for
m<m^{\prime}
, there exists no
t\in\mathcal{T}_{m},t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{m^{\prime}}
with
t\succ t^{\prime}
. The (possibly infinite-dimensional) Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{m}}
can be viewed as internal memory wires. The existence of this representation essentially follows from the Stinespring dilation
[
68
]
of CPTP maps. Appropriate slices can be determined in a recursive fashion via a causality function of the causal box as
\chi(\bigsqcup_{m=1}^{n+1}\mathcal{T}_{m})=\bigsqcup_{m=1}^{n}\mathcal{T}_{m}
.

2.1.4
Composition of causal boxes

Causal boxes are fully compositional under a general set of physical compositions. Indeed, besides the usual parallel and sequential ways of composing CPTP maps, we can also feed the outputs of a causal box back as an input, which is called loop composition. The causality condition then ensures that these looped back outputs cannot affect themselves (or anything outside of their future lightcone).

Definition 4

(Loop composition
[
56
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{AB,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{CD,\mathcal{T}})
be a subnormalised causal box with input systems
A
and
B
and output systems
C
and
D
with
\mathcal{H}^{B}\cong\mathcal{H}^{C}
. Let
\{\ket{k}^{C}\}_{k}
be any orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{F}^{C,\mathcal{T}}
, and denote with
\{\ket{k}^{B}\}_{k}
the corresponding basis of
\mathcal{F}^{B,\mathcal{T}}
i.e. for all
k
,
\ket{k}^{C}\cong\ket{k}^{B}
. The result of looping the output system
C
to the input system
B
is denoted as
\mathcal{C}^{C\hookrightarrow B}
and is given by its Choi-Jamiołkowski representation as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R_{\mathcal{C}^{C\hookrightarrow B}}(\psi^{A}\otimes\psi^{D},\varphi^{A}\otimes\varphi^{D})&=\sum_{k,l}R_{\mathcal{C}}(\psi^{A}\otimes\bar{k}\otimes k\otimes\psi^{D},\varphi^{A}\otimes\bar{l}\otimes l\otimes\varphi^{D})\\
&=\sum_{k,l}\bra{\psi^{D}}\bra{k}\mathcal{C}(\ket{\bar{\psi}^{A}}\bra{\bar{\phi}^{A}}\otimes\ket{k}\bra{l})\ket{l}\ket{\varphi^{D}}\end{aligned}

(10)

The sequential composition of two subnormalised causal boxes
\mathcal{C}_{A}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{A,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{C,\mathcal{T}})
and
\mathcal{C}_{B}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{B,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{D,\mathcal{T}})
can be represented as
(\mathcal{C}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{B})^{C\hookrightarrow B}
. Causal boxes are closed under arbitrary parallel, sequential and loop compositions and the result of composition is invariant under different orders of composition
[
56
,
74
,
75
]
. In particular, this implies that for a (sub)normalised causal box
\mathcal{C}
from inputs
A
and
B
to outputs
C
and
D
, the loop composition
\mathcal{C}^{C\hookrightarrow B}
in the above definition is also a valid (sub)normalised causal box from input
A
to output
D
.

We will generally use the same label to denote isomorphic pairs of input and output systems such as
B
and
C
above, in which case we will use the symbol
\hookrightarrow
as a superscript to denote full loop composition (i.e., loop compose all outputs with matching inputs if and only if such an input exists) of a map
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}^{\hookrightarrow}
, similar to how
\circ
is often used to denote full sequential composition (i.e., sequentially compose all outputs of one map with inputs of the other map if and only if such an input exists). For example, consider two maps
\mathcal{C}_{1}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{A,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{B,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{A,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{C,\mathcal{T}})
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{C,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{D,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{B,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{E,\mathcal{T}})
. Then,
\mathcal{C}_{2}\circ\mathcal{C}_{1}
consists of feeding the output
C
of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
into the matching input
C
of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
and we obtain a map from the systems
A,B,D
to the systems
A,B,E
. On the other hand,
(\mathcal{C}_{2}\circ\mathcal{C}_{1})^{\hookrightarrow}
would additionally loop the output
A
of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
to the input
A
of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and the output
B
of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
to the input
B
of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, ultimately yielding a map from
D
to
E
. Note that for an arbitrary number of maps
\mathcal{C}_{1},...,\mathcal{C}_{N}
, as a consequence of order-independence of composition of causal boxes, loop composition is in particular invariant under cyclic permutations (it only depends on the pairs of systems, one output and one input, being composed)
[
56
,
74
,
75
]
,

(\mathcal{C}_{N}\circ...\circ\mathcal{C}_{1})^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}_{k}\circ\mathcal{C}_{k-1}\circ...\circ\mathcal{C}_{k+1})^{\hookrightarrow}

(11)

for all
k=1,...,N
. If further there exist no two output systems which are the same and no two input systems which are the same among the sets of input respectively output systems of
\mathcal{C}_{1},...,\mathcal{C}_{N}
, we can even unambiguously write

(\mathcal{C}_{N}\circ...\circ\mathcal{C}_{1})^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}_{N}\otimes...\otimes\mathcal{C}_{1})^{\hookrightarrow}

(12)

by using the fact that sequential composition can be expressed in terms of parallel and loop composition. Note that the RHS is ambiguous when multiple input respectively output systems have the same label. For example, for maps
\mathcal{C}_{1}:A\rightarrow A,\mathcal{C}_{2}:A\rightarrow A
, it is ambiguous whether
(\mathcal{C}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{2})^{\hookrightarrow}
refers to
(\mathcal{C}_{2}\circ\mathcal{C}_{1})^{\hookrightarrow}
or
(\mathcal{C}_{1})^{\hookrightarrow}\otimes(\mathcal{C}_{2})^{\hookrightarrow}
.

Instead of working with the sesquilinear form, one can also use the following representation which relies on the CP(TP) maps directly.

Lemma 1

(An alternative representation for loop composition)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{XY,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{YZ,\mathcal{T}})
be a (subnormalised) causal box. Let
\mathcal{T}_{1},...,\mathcal{T}_{M}
be a disjoint partition of
\mathcal{T}
such that
\chi(\bigsqcup_{m=1}^{n+1}\mathcal{T}_{m})=\bigsqcup_{m=1}^{n}\mathcal{T}_{m}
for all
m
for some causality function
\chi
of
\mathcal{C}
. Then,

\mathcal{C}^{\hookrightarrow}(\cdot)=\sum_{ij}\bra{i}^{Y}\mathcal{C}(\cdot\otimes\ket{i}\bra{j}^{Y})\ket{j}^{Y}

(13)

for any basis
\{\ket{i}^{Y}\}
of
\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}}
which is a product basis in terms of the decomposition
\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}}\cong\bigotimes_{m=1}^{M}\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}_{m}}
.

2.2
Higher-order quantum processes

The standard quantum circuit paradigm describes protocols with a well-defined acyclic ordering between the different operations (or quantum gates). This is consistent with a clear arrow of time. Recently, frameworks have been proposed which go beyond this to define abstract information-processing protocols without assuming the existence of a definite acyclic order between the different operations, or the existence of a background spacetime structure
[
6
,
48
,
Chiribella2013
,
78
]
.

Mathematically, a higher-order quantum process or supermap takes a fixed number
N
of CP maps
\mathcal{M}_{A_{k}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k}})
and maps them to another CP map

\mathcal{W}:(\mathcal{M}_{A_{1}},...,\mathcal{M}_{A_{N}})\mapsto\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{P})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{F}).

(14)

These supermaps must satisfy conditions which are analogous to the conditions on CPTP maps. They map CP(TP) maps to a CP(TP) map and they must do so even if the maps
\mathcal{M}_{A_{k}}
are actually extended maps whose input and output both include an arbitrary ancilla. Physically, we think of the maps
\mathcal{M}_{A_{k}}
as modelling the local operations of agents
A_{k}
in their own labs and the application of
\mathcal{W}
models how these combine into a global situation. The mathematical conditions imposed on the supermap ensure some basic consistency. For example, the probability for a given setup is
\text{tr}(\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{M}_{A_{1}},...,\mathcal{M}_{A_{N}})(\rho))
where
\rho\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{P})
and the fact that
\mathcal{W}
maps CPTP maps to CPTP maps ensures that the overall probability of any experiment adds up to 1.

The supermap
\mathcal{W}
can be represented with a Choi-Jamiołkowski matrix, which is usually referred to as a process matrix
[
48
]
. We can then write its action as

W(\mathcal{M}_{A_{1}},...,\mathcal{M}_{A_{N}})\coloneqq(M_{A_{1}}\otimes...\otimes M_{A_{N}})*W\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{PF})

(15)

where
M_{A_{i}}
denotes the Choi-Jamiołkowski matrix of the local operation
\mathcal{M}_{A_{i}}
and
*
is the link product
[
15
,
17
]
. Additionally, we proved in
[
61
]
that the link product can equivalently be expressed using loop composition, hence, we will write the action of supermaps as

\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{M}_{A_{1}},...,\mathcal{M}_{A_{N}})=(\mathcal{W}\circ\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N}\mathcal{M}_{A_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}.

(16)

Note that on the RHS
\mathcal{W}
should be interpreted as the CP(TP) map which is uniquely associated with the supermap.

2.2.1
Quantum circuits with quantum control of causal order

The framework of process matrices describes very general scenarios as no background spacetime is imposed. In particular, some of them go beyond what is achievable through processes compatible with some fixed order and violate causal inequalities
[
48
]
, similar to how some entangled states violate Bell inequalities. However, at the same time, most process matrices currently lack a clear physical interpretation.

Quantum circuits with quantum control of causal order (QC-QC)
[
78
]
are a subclass of process matrices (including those with indefinite causal order) which generalise the notion of a quantum circuit to allow for controlled superpositions of (possibly dynamic) causal orders
5
5

5

A similar framework was developed in
[
58
]
. We will work with the framework of QC-QCs.
. In a QC-QC the causal order of the agents’ operations is either classically or quantumly controlled. The overall action is depicted in
Fig.

2
, illustrating the sense in which these processes can be interpreted as quantum circuits. We will briefly describe the most important features of QC-QCs as well as a mathematical characterisation which will be useful for us. For a detailed introduction we refer to the original work
[
78
]
.

Figure 2
:
Graphical depiction of a quantum circuit with quantum control of causal order (QC-QC). At each step, the QC-QC (shaded pink) applies an internal operation conditioned on some control system (carried on the bottom wire). In each hole an agent operation is plugged in, but it is not fixed which one. Instead, this can depend classically or quantumly on the state of some control or dynamically on the action of previous operations. The figure is taken from
[
78
]
, where it is listed as Fig. 10.

We can imagine a QC-QC to be made up of
N
steps. In any given step, say the
n
-th one, which agent acts is controlled via a system
\mathcal{H}^{C_{n}}
whose basis states take the form
\ket{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}
, where
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
(with
\mathcal{N}=\{1,...,N\}
being the set of all agent labels) is a subset of cardinality
n-1
and
k_{n}\in\mathcal{N}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
. The state
\ket{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}
indicates that the agents labelled by the elements of
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}
have previously acted in some arbitrary order and that agent
k_{n}
is to act in the current step. As the control system can be in quantum superposition this introduces quantum uncertainty into the causal order. In between the agent operations the circuit applies an operation which can itself be conditioned on the control system and which sends the target system to the next agent, say
k_{n+1}
, updating the control in the process to
\ket{\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n},k_{n+1}}
. The internal operations of the circuit can be denoted by the state of the control and the agent the system is being sent to

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} V^{\rightarrow k_{1}}_{\emptyset,\emptyset}&:\mathcal{H}^{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k_{1}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}}\\
V^{\rightarrow k_{n+1}}_{\{k_{1},...,k_{n-1}\},k_{n}}&:\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k_{n}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{n}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k_{n+1}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{n_{1}{}}}\\
V^{\rightarrow F}_{\{k_{1},...,k_{N-1}\},k_{N}}&:\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k_{N}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{N}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{F}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{F}}\end{aligned}

(17)

Here, the systems
\alpha_{n},\alpha_{F}
are ancillary wires which the QC-QC uses to carry information internally. The above maps must then compose into a valid physical transformation for the QC-QC to be valid itself. Mathematically, this means that the maps

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} V_{1}&=\sum_{k_{1}}V^{\rightarrow k_{1}}_{\emptyset,\emptyset}\otimes\ket{\emptyset,k_{1}}\\
V_{n+1}&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n},\\
k_{n+1}\end{subarray}}V^{\rightarrow k_{n+1}}_{\{k_{1},...,k_{n-1}\},k_{n}}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n},k_{n+1}}\bra{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}\\
V_{N+1}&=\sum_{k_{N}}V^{\rightarrow F}_{\mathcal{N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N}}\otimes\bra{\mathcal{K}_{N}\backslash k_{N},k_{N}}\\
\end{aligned}

(18)

define isometries on their effective input space at least, that is on states that can actually be produced by the preceding operations
V_{n}
together with arbitrary agent operations. Indeed, any set of maps as in
Eq.

17
for which this is the case define a QC-QC and any QC-QC admits such a description.

The resulting supermap is then (up to tracing out the purifying degree of freedom
\alpha_{F}
)

(A_{1},...,A_{N})\mapsto\sum_{(k_{1},...,k_{N})}V^{\rightarrow F}_{\{k_{1},...,k_{N-1}\},k_{N}}\circ A_{k_{N}}\circ V^{\rightarrow k_{N}}_{\{k_{1},...,k_{N-2}\},k_{N-1}}...A_{k_{n+1}}\circ V^{\rightarrow k_{n+1}}_{\{k_{1},...,k_{n-1}\},k_{n}}\circ A_{k_{n}}...A_{k_{1}}\circ V^{\rightarrow k_{1}}_{\emptyset,\emptyset}

(19)

where we sum over all possible orders of the agents
(k_{1},...,k_{N})
and
A_{k}:\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k}}
is an arbitrary Kraus operator (note that here we use the notation
A_{k}
for both the agent and the Kraus operator they apply. It should be clear from context to which of the two we are referring in any given case).

3
Multi-partite causal box protocols on finite spacetimes

In this section, we will discuss how to model multipartite information processing protocols using causal boxes. We consider
N
agents
\{A_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}
implementing a protocol in a spacetime
\mathcal{T}
modelled as a partially ordered set with finite cardinality
|\mathcal{T}|
. Note that this does not imply a fundamental discretisation of the spacetime, but simply that we consider protocols with a finite number of information-processing steps. Hence, a finite sample of relevant spacetime locations is sufficient to describe them. An element of
\mathcal{T}
could also represent a small enough spacetime region, instead of a spacetime point, as long as there is a partial order between such regions.
6
6

6

If the regions are too large, bidirectional influences between two regions becomes possible and we no longer have a partial order but rather a pre-order
[
74
,
75
]
.

Agents’ operations
To each agent
A_{k}
, we will associate a set of input positions
\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
and a set of output positions
\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
as well as a set of causal boxes describing the agent’s allowed operations
\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}}
, where
x_{k}
is a classical setting that parametrises the choice of operation. Note that the set of allowed
x_{k}
here need not be finite, hence these sets of allowed operations are not necessarily finite, or even countable. In particular, the set
\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}}
could include all valid causal boxes. Each allowed operation is therefore a causal box of the form

\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{A^{O}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}\otimes(\mathcal{H}^{R_{k}^{x_{k}}}\otimes\ket{T}))

(20)

where we conceptualise the wire
R_{k}^{x_{k}}
as carrying the agents’ measurement outcome in the future, i.e.,
T\succeq t
for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
. We allow this wire, in particular its dimension, to depend on the choice of operation for generality (indicated by the superscript
x_{k}
). This form is w.l.o.g, as long as agents cannot act on each other’s outcome wires, which is indeed what will be ensured in the framework for capturing that agents act within separate closed labs. Even if we considered multiple outcome wires with different spacetime positions, we could equivalently post-process them to a single outcome at
T
. We note a related work
[
24
]
where the explicit internal modelling of
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
, leading to two distinct measurement models for agents in spacetime, is explored. While we do not explore these details here, our model here is general and consistent with both those measurement models.

Furthermore, while we do not model the outcome wire as a Fock space, we could always embed it into one, hence, it still makes sense to call
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
a causal box. We can write

\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{a_{k}|x_{k}}:=\bra{a_{k},T}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\ket{a_{k},T}

(21)

corresponding to the outcome
a_{k}
. The map
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{a_{k}|x_{k}}
is thus a subnormalised causal box.

Multi-partite causal box
The interaction between different agents will be mediated by what we call a multi-partite causal box, which is a causal box of the form

\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{P,t_{P}}\otimes\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N}\mathcal{F}^{A^{O}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N}\mathcal{F}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{F,t_{F}})

(22)

from the outputs of a set of
N
agents and a global past system
P
, to the inputs of the same
N
agents together with a global future system
F
. The past and future spaces are taken to carry messages of dimension
d_{P}
and
d_{F}
with fixed spacetime labels
t_{P}
and
t_{F}
, with
t_{P}\prec t\prec t_{F}\prec T
for all other
t\in\mathcal{T}\backslash\{t_{P},t_{F},T\}
. Note that this too can ultimately be seen as a causal box with a single input and output wire at all spacetime positions in
\mathcal{T}
by using
Eq.

3
to append vacuum and then the wire isomorphism
Eq.

2
. Notice that a multi-partite process box is analogous to the channel representation of a process matrix which is a channel from the outputs of all agents and the global past to the inputs of all agents and the global future.

Definition 5

(Multi-partite causal box protocols)
.

A multi-partite causal box protocol is a tuple
\mathfrak{P}:=(\mathcal{C},(\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=1}^{N},\{\mathcal{M}^{P}_{x_{p}}\}_{x_{p}},\{\mathcal{M}^{F}_{x_{f}}\}_{x_{f}})
specified by a multi-partite causal box
\mathcal{C}
together with a set of causal boxes
\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}}
as defined in
Eq.

20
for each agent
\{A_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}
along with sets of causal boxes
\{\mathcal{M}^{P}_{x_{P}}\}_{x_{P}}
and
\{\mathcal{M}^{F}_{x_{F}}\}_{x_{F}}
for the global past and future agents, where the maps
\mathcal{M}^{P}_{x_{P}}
have a trivial input space along with output space
P
and the result space
R_{P}^{x_{P}}
, while for the maps of the global future we have

\mathcal{M}^{F}_{x_{F}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{F,t_{F}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{R_{F}^{x_{F}}}\otimes\ket{T}).

(23)

In the following, we will denote the past and future agents as the
k=0^{th}
and
k=N+1^{th}
agents and simply write
\mathfrak{P}=(\mathcal{C},(\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
for the protocol involving
N+2
agents,
\{A_{0},...,A_{N+1}\}
.

Composition
The composition of all the maps in a multi-partite causal box protocol
\mathfrak{P}
(obtained by connecting wires with matching system and spacetime labels via loop composition) defines a state on the result wires
\mathcal{L}(\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{H}^{R_{k}^{x_{k}}}\otimes\ket{T})

(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}\in\mathcal{L}(\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{H}^{R_{k}^{x_{k}}}\otimes\ket{T})

(24)

or a probability, when using the subnormalised causal boxes
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{a_{k}|x_{k}}
for agents:

P(a_{0},...,a_{N+1}|x_{0},...,x_{N+1})=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{a_{k}|x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}

(25)

The causal box framework guarantees that arbitrary composition of causal boxes via connecting systems with such matching labels yields a valid causal box, which implies that the above must be a valid probability distribution for any multi-partite causal box and agents’ operations defined on corresponding spaces.

Figure 3
:
A trivial violation of a causal inequality. The label on each wire indicates the variable that wire carries at that point. Alice and Bob receive an input bit
x
respectively
y
. Alice sends her bit to Bob who swaps the two bits
x,y
. Finally, Alice acts a second time and outputs the bit she received from Bob.

Despite the structural similarities in the constructions so far, arbitrary causal box protocols should not be thought of as processes as the following example shows.

Example 1

(Trivial causal inequality violation)
.

We give an example of a bipartite causal box protocol (illustrated in
Fig.

3
) which maximally violates the causal inequality obtained from the so-called guess your neighbour’s input game (GYNI)
[
10
]
. In this game, Alice receives a bit
x
, outputs a bit
a
, Bob receives a bit
y
, outputs a bit
b
and the goal for them is that
x=b
and
y=a
. This game gives rise to a causal inequality under certain assumptions, more precisely, if the agents act within closed labs (acting once with inputs preceding outputs) and choose operations freely, then for any causally ordered strategy of Alice and Bob, their winning probability is at most
\frac{1}{2}
.

We will define the action of the agents and the causal box only on states which can actually occur in the protocol for simplicity. We assume that Alice can only receive an input at
t=4
while Bob can only receive an input at
t=2
and that all states are classical. The agents’ operations are then

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{M}^{A}_{x}(\ket{y,t=4}\bra{y,t=4}^{A^{I}})&=\ket{x,t=1}\bra{x,t=1}^{A^{O}}\otimes\ket{y,t=5}\bra{y,t=5}^{R_{A}}\\
\mathcal{M}^{B}_{y}(\ket{x,t=2}\bra{x,t=2}^{B^{I}})&=\ket{y,t=3}\bra{y,t=3}^{B^{O}}\otimes\ket{x,t=5}\bra{x,t=5}^{R_{B}}.\end{aligned}

(26)

The causal box acts as

\mathcal{C}(\ket{x,t=1}\bra{x,t=1}^{A^{O}}\otimes\ket{y,t=3}\bra{y,t=3}^{B^{O}})=\ket{x,t=2}\bra{x,t=2}^{B^{I}}\otimes\ket{y,t=4}\bra{y,t=4}^{A^{I}}.

(27)

Composing these yields

(\mathcal{C}\circ(\mathcal{M}^{A}_{x}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{B}_{y}))^{\hookrightarrow}=\ket{y,t=5}\bra{y,t=5}^{R_{A}}\otimes\ket{x,t=5}\bra{x,t=5}^{R_{B}}

(28)

and we see that Alice and Bob win the GYNI game with certainty.

This causal inequality violation is, however, not a witness of indefinite causal order. The protocol is perfectly compatible with a well-defined causal order, simply not one where each agent acts once and only once with their inputs happening before their outputs, i.e., this violates the closed labs assumption. Indeed, from
Eq.

26
, we see that Alice sends out a message at
t=1
before she receives one at
t=4
.

Multipartite causal box protocols model multipartite quantum experiments in spacetime. Two experimenters can make different choices in their experimental design, for example, they might choose different physical systems, different measurement apparatuses, or even just when and where they carry out their experiment. Still, if the observations we care about are the same in both, we would still think of their experiments as being equivalent in terms of their relevant behaviour. We formalise this in the definition below. This will in particular become relevant when we connect causal box protocols to QC-QCs. There may be many ways in which an abstract QC-QC can be physically realised in spacetime, each such realisation corresponding to some causal box protocol with behavioural equivalence among the different realisations of the same QC-QC.

Definition 6

(Behavioural equivalence of causal boxes)
.

Consider two
N
-partite causal box protocols,
\mathfrak{P}=(\mathcal{C},(\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
and
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},(\{\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
. We say that
\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
are behaviourally equivalent if for all
k\in\{0,...,N+1\}
and for each agent operation
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
, there exists an agent operation
\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
, and vice versa, such that

(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}.

(29)

4
Defining process boxes compositionally

4.1
Definition

As we have seen in the previous section, general causal boxes can violate causal inequalities trivially via violations of the closed labs assumption of the process matrix/higher-order frameworks. Hence, we cannot think of arbitrary causal box protocols as “processes” in the same sense of the higher-order framework. The goal of this section is to define the subset of causal box protocols which do in fact deserve to be called processes, by formalising the closed labs assumption within the context of such spacetime protocols. An initial definition has previously been developed in
[
Vilasini_2020
]
which first proposed the idea of
process boxes
. Here, we will build on that work, while fully formalising and generalising the definition of process boxes in the process.

An important aspect of processes is that each agent acts exactly once, with an implicit local order: inputs are received before producing relevant outputs. Ignoring this assumption leads to trivial causal inequality violation as seen in
Example

1
. We first formalise the idea of an agent acting once as there being only a single message on their input and output wire. For this purpose, let us first define the 1-message subspace and the associated projector onto that space.

Definition 7

(1-message subspace supported in a spacetime region)
.

Consider a spacetime
\mathcal{T}
. Let
A_{k}
be an agent with non-trivial input/output space and corresponding Fock space
\mathcal{F}^{A^{I/O}_{k},\mathcal{T}}
. For any subset
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
, the
1-message subspace supported in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is the subspace of
\mathcal{F}^{A^{I/O}_{k},\mathcal{T}}
consisting of states with
exactly one message located somewhere in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
and vacuum at all other spacetime locations in
\mathcal{T}
. This space can be written as

\mathcal{H}^{A^{I/O}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{H}^{A^{I/O}_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subsetneq\mathcal{F}^{A^{I/O}_{k},\mathcal{T}}

(30)

which is naturally embedded in the full Fock space by identifying

\ket{\psi,t}\mapsto\ket{\psi,t}\otimes\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}\backslash\{t\}}.

(31)

The projector onto this space from the full Fock space on
\mathcal{T}
is thus well-defined and can be denoted as

\mathcal{P}^{I/O,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}_{k}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{A^{I/O}_{k},\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{I/O}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}).

(32)

If
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}
, we will also denote this projector as
\mathcal{P}^{I/O}_{k}
. If
A_{k}
has a trivial in/output, then the corresponding projector
\mathcal{P}^{I/O,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}_{k}
is defined to be trivial for all
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
(i.e., simply acts as the identity on the trivial space).

Note that
P^{I/O,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}_{k}
acts on the full Fock space over
\mathcal{T}
. It projects onto states with exactly one message somewhere in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
and vacuum on all other spacetime locations in
\mathcal{T}
. These projectors should not be interpreted as acting independently on subspaces associated with individual locations. In particular, an expression like
\mathcal{P}^{I,t}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{I,t^{\prime}}_{k}
is not well-defined.

In general, we should not think of projectors on the Fock space as physical operations as they can allow backwards in time signalling (an explicit example is given in
Example

2
). However, in the case of the projectors in
Eq.

32
it turns out that we can interpret them as subnormalised pseudo-causal boxes, which we define as follows: A pseudo-causal box is essentially a causal box but processing can be instantaneous (this relaxes the condition that
\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\subsetneq\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
for valid causality functions on bottom-closed sets
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
to allow
\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
). A formal definition is given in
14
in
Section

A.1
. There, we also show that composing either all the input or all the output wires of pseudo-causal boxes with a causal box yields a causal box (
Lemma

7
). This means that we can think of pseudo-causal boxes as physical operations where the processing time is allowed to go to 0 (in contrast to causal boxes where this must be strictly non-zero).

Lemma 2
.

The projectors
\mathcal{P}^{I,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}_{k}
and
\mathcal{P}^{I,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}_{k}
are subnormalised pseudo-causal boxes for all
k
and all
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
.

Physical intuition on 1-message projectors and subspaces
We now discuss an equivalent way to understand these projectors, which arises through the wire isomorphism. This serves to provide further physical intuition and to show consistency with related literature on causal/process boxes, in particular the initial process box proposal of
[
Vilasini_2020
]
as well as our previous work
[
61
]
and a related work involving one of us
[
24
]
. Moreover, this is also relevant for connecting to the routed circuit formalism (and its sectorial constraints)
[
72
]
and the analysis of the fine-grained quantum switch in
[
73
]
.

Note that the 1-message space for any system
S
of dimension
d_{S}
associated with spacetime locations
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, is by definition equivalent to a direct sum of 1-message spaces of
S
associated to each location
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}

\mathcal{H}^{S,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{H}^{S}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{|\mathcal{T}^{\prime}|}=\bigoplus_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{S}\otimes\ket{t}=\bigoplus_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{S,t}=\text{span}\Big(\{\ket{i,t}\}_{i\in\{0,...,d_{S}-1\},t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\Big)

(33)

Moreover, this is isomorphic to a subspace of a tensor product of spaces associated to each location
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, where each space in the product can contain a vacuum (zero-message) or a single non-vacuum (1-message) state

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{H}^{S,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\cong\text{span}\Big(\{\ket{i,t}\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\{t\}}\}_{i\in\{0,...,d_{S}-1\},t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\Big)\subseteq\bigotimes_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\Big((\mathcal{H}^{S}\oplus\ket{\Omega})\otimes\ket{t}\Big).\end{split}

(34)

Finally, by the trivial embedding in
Eq.

3
, we also have for any
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
,

\text{span}\Big(\{\ket{i,t}\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\{t\}}\}_{i\in\{0,...,d_{S}-1\},t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\Big)\cong\text{span}\Big(\{\ket{i,t}\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\{t\}}\}_{i\in\{0,...,d_{S}-1\},t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\Big)\otimes\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}^{S}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{S,\mathcal{T}}

(35)

Therefore note that in particular, we can equivalently express the 1-message projector for any spacetime location
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\{t\}
as the projector on the subspace
\text{span}\Big(\{\ket{i,t}\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}\backslash\{t\}}\}_{i\in\{0,...,d_{S}-1\}}\Big)
.

Example 2

(Signalling measurements)
.

Consider the Fock space
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{T}})
where
\mathcal{T}=\{1,2\}
and the projector
\mathcal{P}_{\leq 1}=P_{\leq 1}(\cdot)P_{\leq 1}
, where
P_{\leq 1}
projects onto the subspace containing 0 or 1 messages,
(\mathbb{C}^{2}\oplus\ket{\Omega})\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{T}}
. This projector allows for backwards-in-time signalling, no matter how we choose other orthogonal projectors
\{\mathcal{P}_{i}\}_{i}
to obtain a complete measurement

\text{tr}_{t=2}\circ(\mathcal{P}_{\leq 1}+\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}_{i})\neq\text{tr}_{t=2}\circ(\mathcal{P}_{\leq 1}+\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}_{i})\circ\text{tr}_{t=2},

(36)

that is, the choice of input state at
t=2
can influence the output state at the earlier time
t=1
. To see this, consider the two states
(\ket{\Omega,t=1}+\ket{0,t=1})\ket{\Omega,t=2}/\sqrt{2}
and
(\ket{\Omega,t=1}+\ket{0,t=1})\ket{0,t=2}/\sqrt{2}
. Tracing out the
t=2
time stamp yields the same state at
t=1
,
(\ket{\Omega,t=1}+\ket{0,t=1})/\sqrt{2}
, and so applying the measurement to these two states and then tracing out the
t=2
time stamp should yield the same state. The first state lies in the image of
\mathcal{P}_{\leq 1}
, hence, the measurement acts trivially on it and after tracing out the
t=2
time stamp, we obtain the pure state
(\ket{\Omega,t=1}+\ket{0,t=1})/\sqrt{2}
. For the second state, we find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{tr}_{tr=2}\circ(\mathcal{P}_{\leq 1}&+\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}_{i})((\ket{\Omega,t=1}+\ket{0,t=1})(\bra{\Omega,t=1}+\bra{0,t=1})\otimes\ket{0,t=2}\bra{0,t=2}/2)\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\ket{\Omega,t=1}\bra{\Omega,t=1}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\text{tr}_{t=2}\circ\mathcal{P}_{i}(\ket{0,t=1}\bra{0,t=1}\otimes\ket{0,t=2}\bra{0,t=2}).\end{aligned}

(37)

This is either a mixed state or equal to vacuum at
t=1
. In both cases, it is different from
(\ket{\Omega,t=1}+\ket{0,t=1})/\sqrt{2}
and so we observe signalling from
t=2
to
t=1
, backwards in time.

With the projectors of
7
, we can now formalise the notion of each agent acting exactly once.

Definition 8

(Acting once condition for a causal box protocol)
.

Given a causal box protocol
\mathfrak{P}
we say that
\mathfrak{P}
satisfies acting once (AO) if for all
x_{k}

(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}(\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}))^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}

(38)

Figure 4
:
Graphical depiction of the Action Once (AO) condition of
8
. The projectors
\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k},\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}
can be viewed as physical operations (
Lemma

2
), corresponding to measuring the number of messages on an agent’s input respectively output wire over the entire experimental procedure and getting the outcome “1 message”. The condition then states that this measurement is non-disturbing, verifying that the agent only received exactly a single message and sent exactly a single message.

This condition is illustrated graphically in
Fig.

4
. In other words, in the causal box protocol, it is guaranteed that each agent receives and sends one message at some spacetime location in
\mathcal{T}
(or more precisely, in
\mathcal{T}^{I/O}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
for agent
A_{k}
) or a superposition thereof.

Note that the above condition also restricts the global past and future to their respective one-message spaces. In particular, this restricts the global past’s operations, which for general causal box protocols are states in the Fock space, to states in the 1-message space. The projectors
\mathcal{P}^{I}_{0}
and
\mathcal{P}^{O}_{N+1}
are trivial since the past input and future output spaces are trivial.

Lemma

2
implies that we can interpret the AO condition in an operational manner. There exists an operational method (corresponding to the pseudo-causal boxes of which the projectors
\mathcal{P}^{I/O}_{k}
are a particular outcome
7
7

7

The pseudo-causal box adds the idealisation that this measurement can be instantaneous, this is merely a simplification that allows to preserve the input/output time stamps of the original causal box protocol.
) to check if a protocol satisfies AO during a run of the protocol. This procedure is moreover non-disturbing if AO is satisfied.

Intuitively, one would expect that if a causal box protocol satisfies AO, then only checking the number of messages on the input wires, but not the number of messages on the output wires (or vice versa), should not disturb the protocol either. The below lemma shows that this is true. We note that the physicality of the projectors
\mathcal{P}^{I/O}_{k}
is an important ingredient here as an analogous result does not hold for projectors which are not subnormalised pseudo-causal boxes (as seen in
Example

2
).

Lemma 3
.

If a causal box protocol satisfies AO, then for all
x_{k}

(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}(\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}))^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}))^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}(\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}))^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}

(39)

Proof.

This is a consequence of
5
with the sequential composition of
\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}(\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})
playing the role of the causal box and
\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}
playing the role of the projector (and analogously, when switching
I
and
O
).
∎

As we have seen in
Example

1
, another way we can trivially violate causal inequalities (using a causally ordered protocol) is by allowing agents to send a non-vacuum message before they receive a non-vacuum message. Indeed, in the higher-order processes framework, one assumes that there is a local causal ordering inside each agent’s lab, with their output coming after their input (both of these being non-vacuum by definition), even if the overall causal order between different agents’ operations is not specified.

To unambiguously formalise the condition for causal box protocols, we will consider situations where if an agent
A_{k}
receives some (non-vacuum) message at an input location
t\in\mathcal{T}_{k}^{I}
they will output a (non-vacuum) message at a unique later output location
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{k}^{O}
,
t^{\prime}\succ t
, such that no two input locations associate to the same output location. This correspondence between input and output locations is a restriction, although it is physically well motivated and satisfied in particular in all scenarios where each agent’s device has a fixed processing time translating inputs at time
t
to outputs at
t+\Delta
for
\Delta>0

[
74
,
75
]
. The relaxation of this restriction is discussed later in
Section

7
, and entails open questions.

We wish to ultimately formalise the above intuition in a manner similar to AO, where it only needs to hold effectively during an actual run of the protocol. As a first step, however, we present a simpler formulation that refers only to the agent operations.

Figure 5
:
Graphical depiction of the Agent Local Order (ALO) condition of
9
.
This tells us that whenever
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
receives a single message at input position
t
(and no other non-vacuum inputs in
\mathcal{T}_{k}^{I}
) as ensured by the input projector
\mathcal{P}^{I,t}_{k}
, it is guaranteed to output a single non-vacuum message at a unique later time
\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)\succ t
(and no other non-vacuum outputs in
\mathcal{T}_{k}^{O}
) as ensured by the output projector
\mathcal{P}^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}_{k}
.

Definition 9

(Agent local order condition for a causal box protocol)
.

For an agent
A_{k}
with non-trivial input and output spaces, we say that an agent operation
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
satisfies Agent Local Order (ALO) if there exists an injective map
\mathcal{O}_{k}:\mathcal{T}_{k}^{I}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{k}^{O}
with
\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)\succ t
for all
t\in\mathcal{T}_{k}^{I}
such that for all
t\in\mathcal{T}

\mathcal{P}^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I,t}_{k}=\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I,t}_{k}.

(40)

We say that the agent
A_{k}
with non-trivial input and output spaces satisfies ALO if all their operations satisfy ALO with the same map
\mathcal{O}_{k}
. We say that a causal box protocol satisfies ALO if all its agents with non-trivial input and output systems satisfy ALO.

We also graphically illustrate this definition in
Fig.

5
.

We now generalise this definition to a condition called Local Order (LO) that involves the whole causal box protocol (more analogous to the Acting Once or AO condition) as opposed to just the agent operations as in Agent Local Order or ALO (see also the graphical depiction of LO in
Fig.

6
).

Definition 10

(Local order condition for a causal box protocol)
.

Given a causal box protocol
\mathfrak{P}
, we say that
\mathfrak{P}
satisfies local order (LO) if for each agent
A_{k}
with non-trivial input and output spaces, there exists an injective map
\mathcal{O}_{k}:\mathcal{T}_{k}^{I}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{k}^{O}
with
\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)\succ t
for all
t\in\mathcal{T}_{k}^{I}
such that for all
x_{k}

(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}))^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}))^{\hookrightarrow}

(41)

where

\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})(\rho):=\sum_{t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}P^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}_{k}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}(P^{I,t}_{k}\rho P^{I,t^{\prime}}_{k})P^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}

(42)

if the input and output spaces of
A_{k}
are non-trivial.

If the output space is trivial we instead have
\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})=\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}_{k}^{I}
and if the input space is trivial, then
\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})=\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
.

This effective definition is indeed more general, i.e., a weaker assumption than ALO as shown in the following lemma.

Lemma 4

(ALO implies LO)
.

If
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
satisfies ALO, then

\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}=\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}).

(43)

This implies that if
\mathfrak{P}=(\mathcal{C},(\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
is a causal box protocol satisfying AO and ALO, then
\mathfrak{P}
is a process box protocol (satisfies AO and LO).

The object
\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}
can be rewritten in terms of an encoding and decoding operation which are subnormalised pseudo-causal boxes connected by a memory which is a causal box, giving it a well-defined operational interpretation (it can be viewed as a higher-order operation acting on the agents’ operation as shown in
Fig.

6
). Concretely, it can be seen as applying the subnormalised pseudo-causal box
\mathcal{P}^{I}_{LO,k}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}})
which is defined via a single Kraus operator

\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}P^{I,t}_{k}\otimes\ket{0,t}^{\alpha}

(44)

followed by
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\otimes\sum_{t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}\bra{t}\cdot\ket{t^{\prime}}^{\alpha}\bra{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t^{\prime})}^{\alpha}
and finally the subnormalised pseudo-causal box
\mathcal{P}^{O}_{LO,k}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{A^{O}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{A^{O}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}})
which is defined via a single Kraus operator

\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}P^{O,t}_{k}\otimes\bra{0,t}^{\alpha}.

(45)

Summarising this in one equation, we have

\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})=\mathcal{P}^{I}_{LO,k}\circ(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{k})\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{LO,k}.

(46)

where we abused notation to write
\mathcal{O}_{k}=\sum_{t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}\bra{t}\cdot\ket{t^{\prime}}^{\alpha}\bra{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t^{\prime})}^{\alpha}
.

That these maps are indeed subnormalised pseudo-causal boxes is shown in
Lemma

13
in
Section

A.1
.

Figure 6
:
Graphical depiction of the Local Order (LO) condition of
10
. The projectors
\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}
act on the agents’ operations. This operation can be decomposed into a comb-like structure involving two subnormalised pseudo-causal boxes (
\mathcal{P}^{I/O}_{LO,k}
) and a causal box
\mathcal{O}_{k}
which simply maps messages at time
t
to messages at time
\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)
. It can thus be viewed as a higher-order transformation acting on the agents’ operation, as shown in the middle figure. The condition ensures that during the protocol, the agents’ operations do not send out a non-vacuum message before receiving a non-vacuum message from the multi-partite causal box
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 11

(Process box protocols)
.

We call a multipartite causal box protocol
\mathfrak{P}
a process box protocol if
\mathfrak{P}
satisfies AO and LO.

Combining
10
and
Lemma

3
, we immediately find that a process box protocol satisfies

(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}))^{\hookrightarrow}.

(47)

Here we have defined process box protocols in terms of LO rather than ALO as the former is weaker, and thus allows for a larger class of protocols to qualify as process box protocols. However, in the next section we will show that any protocol satisfying LO is behaviourally equivalent to one satisfying ALO.

Effective Choi
A multipartite causal box
\mathcal{C}
acts on an infinite dimensional Fock space and its Choi representation is therefore generically given by a sesquilinear form
[
56
]
. However, when we know that this causal box
\mathcal{C}
is in fact part of a process box protocol
\mathfrak{P}
, the AO and LO restrictions enable us to write an effective, finite-dimensional Choi representation of
\mathcal{C}
which can be equivalently used whenever we consider its behaviour under composition with the agents’ allowed operations in this protocol. For this, we define the effective Hilbert space of the joint input and output of such a
\mathcal{C}
which is identified via the AO and LO restrictions

\mathcal{H}_{eff}=\bigotimes_{k}\bigoplus_{t\in\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},t}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k},\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)})\subsetneq\bigotimes_{k}\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k},\mathcal{T}}\subsetneq\bigotimes_{k}\mathcal{F}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{A^{O}_{k},\mathcal{T}}

(48)

where
\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k},\succ t}=\bigoplus_{t^{\prime}\succ t}\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k},t^{\prime}}
.

For a process box protocol, we can then write (notice that in the below all three expressions are density matrices in
\mathcal{L}(\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{H}^{R^{x_{k}}_{k},T})
and the equation is thus well-typed)

(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}))^{\hookrightarrow}=C_{eff}*\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}M^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}})}

(49)

where
M^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}})}
is the Choi-Jamiołkowski matrix of the restriction to the one-message space of
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
(equivalently, of
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}
) and we call
C_{eff}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{eff})
the effective Choi-Jamiołkowski of
\mathcal{C}
and it is defined as the Choi-Jamiołkowski matrix of the effective causal box

\mathcal{C}_{eff}(\cdot):=\sum_{\{t_{k},t^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}\}_{k}}\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}P^{I,t_{k}}_{k}\mathcal{C}(\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}P^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t_{k})}_{k}\cdot\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}P^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t_{k}^{\prime})})\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}P^{I,t_{k}^{\prime}}_{k}.

(50)

Note that the effective causal box can be viewed as a map on a finite-dimensional space and hence its Choi-Jamiołkowski matrix is well-defined.
Equation

49
can be verified straightforwardly by writing
(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}))^{\hookrightarrow}
with the alternative representation for loop composition of
Lemma

1
and the equivalence of loop composition and the link product in the finite-dimensional setting
[
61
]
.

The conditions AO and LO above are both properties of the CB protocol as a whole (involving both the CB and the agents’ operations) and are effective, in the sense, that they only care about what happens when we compose the protocol.
