If any of the maps are subnormalised, we can instead work with the underlying normalised map. Hence, it is sufficient to prove the statement for normalised (pseudo)-causal boxes.

Since all maps are CPTP, their compositions are also CPTP. Hence, we only need to check that the composed maps respect (pseudo)-causality (
Eq.

6
or respectively
Eq.

81
). Let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
be an arbitrary bottom-closed set. For the parallel composition, note

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{C})&=(\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\otimes(\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C})\\
&=(\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}})\otimes(\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}})\\
&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{C})\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\end{aligned}

(90)

where we used the pseudo-causality of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and that the trace factorises over subsystems.

For sequential composition, let
\chi
(
\chi^{\prime}
) be a causality function for
\mathcal{C}
(
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
) if it is a causal box and identity if it is a pseudo-causal box. Then,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{C}&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\chi^{\prime}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})}\circ\mathcal{C}\\
&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\chi^{\prime}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\chi(\chi^{\prime}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime}))}\\
&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\chi(\chi^{\prime}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime}))}.\end{aligned}

(91)

Here, we used (pseudo)-causality of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in each line. If
\chi,\chi^{\prime}
are both trivial (i.e., both
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
are pseudo-causal boxes), this is the pseudo-causality condition. If either of them is an actual causality function, then this is the causality condition and
\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is a causal box.
∎

It is often more convenient to work with pure maps instead of CPTP maps. While the Stinespring dilation guarantees the existence of purifications of (pseudo)-causal boxes it is a priori unclear whether these are (pseudo)-causal boxes themselves (we can say that an isometry
V
is a (pseudo)-causal box if the CPTP map
V\cdot V^{\dagger}
is a (pseudo)-causal box). The lemma below shows that we can always find purifications where this is true.

Lemma 8

(Purifications of causal boxes)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}})
be (pseudo-)causal box. Let
V:\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\alpha,T}
be a purification of
\mathcal{C}
such that
T\succ t
for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
. Then,
V
is a (pseudo-)causal box. Moreover, such purifications exist for any choice of the finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
, in particular, when
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}=\mathbb{C}
is trivial.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{C}
is a CPTP map it has a purification
V^{\prime}:\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Due to the existence of minimal purifications where the cardinality of the purifying system is at most that of the input Hilbert space, we can choose
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime}}
to be an infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert space. As all such spaces are isomorphic, for any finite-dimensional
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
, there exists a unitary isomorphism
U:\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\alpha,T}
and
V:=U\circ V^{\prime}
is again a purification of
\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, it is a (pseudo-)causal box. To see this, let
\chi
be a causality function for
\mathcal{C}
if
\mathcal{C}
is a causal box or identity if
\mathcal{C}
is a pseudo-causal box and let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
be a bottom-closed set (note that
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is then also a bottom-closed set in
\mathcal{T}\cup T
),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{tr}_{(\mathcal{T}\cup T)\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}V&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\text{tr}_{T}V\\
&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\text{tr}_{\alpha}V\\
&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C}\\
&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})}\\
&=\text{tr}_{(\mathcal{T}\cup T)\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\circ V\circ\text{tr}_{(\mathcal{T}\cup T)\backslash\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})}\end{aligned}

(92)

where we used (pseudo)-causality of
\mathcal{C}
, that
V
is a purification of
\mathcal{C}
and that there is no input time
T
, so we can freely trace over it.
∎

Lemma 9

(A pseudo-causal box into the one-message space)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}^{X}
be a finite-dimensional Hilbert space and
\mathcal{T}
a finite totally ordered spacetime. There exists a pseudo-causal box
\mathcal{C}_{one}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})
which acts as identity on the one-message space and whose image is the one-message space.

Proof.

W.l.o.g., we assume
\mathcal{T}=\{1,...,M\}
. Let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}:=\{M+1,...,2M\}
. We define
V_{one}:\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
via its action on the spanning set of states
\{\ket{\psi_{n},t}\otimes\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}>t}\ket{\phi_{t^{\prime}},t^{\prime}}|t\in\mathcal{T},\ket{\psi_{n}}\in\vee^{n}\mathcal{H}^{X},n\in\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}\}
as follows

V_{one}\ket{\psi_{n},t}\otimes\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}>t}\ket{\phi_{t^{\prime}},t^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\ket{\psi_{1},t}\otimes\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}>t}\ket{\phi_{t^{\prime}},t+M}\otimes\ket{1}^{\alpha},&n=1\\
\ket{0,M}\otimes\ket{\psi_{n},t+M}\otimes\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}>t}\ket{\phi_{t^{\prime}},t^{\prime}+M}\otimes\ket{1}^{\alpha},&n>1\\
\ket{0,M}\otimes\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\otimes\ket{0}^{\alpha},&n=0\end{cases}

(93)

This is an isometry. The three cases in the definition above are the action of
V_{one}
on orthogonal subspaces. Restricted to each of these, it clearly acts as an isometry. Hence, we only need to check that it keeps these subspaces orthogonal. This is clear when comparing the case
n=0
to the other two due to the system
\alpha
. Considering the case
n=1
, we see that if
t<M
, then the output has vacuum at
M
, and is thus orthogonal to any state obtained in the
n>1
case. If
t=M
, then the input state must be
\ket{\psi_{1},t=M}
and the output has vacuum on
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
while in the
n>1
case we always have non-vacuum on
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.

This is a pseudo-causal box (once we trace out
\alpha
). For some
r\in\mathcal{T}
, we can explicitly rewrite

V_{one}=V_{2}\circ V_{1}

(94)

where
V_{1}
is simply the restriction of
V_{one}
to time steps less or equal
r
and
V_{2}:\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}^{>r}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}^{>r}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
(where
\mathcal{T}^{>r}=\{r+1,...,M\}
) acts as

V_{2}(\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}>r}\ket{\psi_{t^{\prime}},t^{\prime}}^{X}\otimes\ket{i}^{\alpha})=\begin{cases}V_{one}|_{\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}^{>r}}}\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}>r}\ket{\psi_{t^{\prime}},t^{\prime}},&i=0\\
\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}>r}\ket{\psi_{t^{\prime}},t^{\prime}+M}\otimes\ket{1}^{\alpha},&i=1\end{cases}

(95)

Both
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are isometries. For
V_{1}
this is obvious as it is a restriction of an isometry. For
V_{2}
, notice first that the two cases in the definition correspond to the action of
V_{2}
on orthogonal subspaces and the map is an isometry when restricted to either of them. The map
V_{2}
maps these orthogonal subspaces to orthogonal subspaces, as in the
i=0
case we have non-vacuum on the output in
\mathcal{T}
while in the
i=1
case, we have vacuum on the output in
\mathcal{T}
.

Hence, we have

\text{tr}_{>r}\text{tr}_{\alpha}V_{one}=(\text{tr}V_{2})\circ\text{tr}_{>r}V_{1}=\text{tr}_{\alpha}\text{tr}_{>r}V_{1}\circ\text{tr}_{>r}

(96)

where we used that
V_{2}
is an isometry, i.e., in particular trace-preserving. As this can be done for any
r\in\mathcal{T}
,
\text{tr}_{\alpha}V_{one}
defines a pseudo-causal box.

The pseudo-causal box
\mathcal{C}_{one}:=\text{tr}_{\alpha}\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}V_{one}
now satisfies the conditions in the lemma statement as can be seen straightforwardly from the definition of
V_{one}
.
∎

Lemma 10

(Extending maps to causal boxes)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{X,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{Y,\mathcal{T}})
be a CPTP map such that
\mathcal{H}^{X},\mathcal{H}^{Y}
are finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces and
\mathcal{T}
is totally ordered and finite and it holds that

\mathcal{C}\ket{\psi,t}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{Y,\geq t})

(97)

for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
and all
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}^{X}
. Then, there exists a pseudo-causal box
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}})
such that

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{X,\mathcal{T}})}=\mathcal{C}

(98)

and the image of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
lies in the 1-message space, i.e.,

\text{Im}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{Y,\mathcal{T}}).

(99)

If

\mathcal{C}\ket{\psi,t}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{Y,>t})

(100)

for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
and all
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}^{X}
, then
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
can be chosen to be a causal box.

Proof.

We define
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}_{one}
where
\mathcal{C}_{one}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})
is the pseudo-causal box whose image is the one-message space and which acts as identity on the one-message space (the existence of such pseudo-causal boxes is shown in
Lemma

9
). This is well-defined as the image of
\mathcal{C}_{one}
is the one-message space, satisfies
Eq.

98
as
\mathcal{C}_{one}
acts as identity on the one-message space and
Eq.

99
as
\mathcal{C}
maps into the one-message space.

Hence, we only need that this is a (pseudo-)causal box. Clearly, it is CPTP as the composition of CPTP maps is CPTP again. We now check the (pseudo)-causality condition. Let
V:\mathcal{H}^{X,\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{Y,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
be a purification of
\mathcal{C}
. For
r\in\mathcal{T}
, we can write (up to restricting the RHS to the one-message space)

V=V_{2}\circ V_{1}

(101)

where
V_{1}=(\ket{\Omega,<r}\bra{\Omega,<r}^{X}\otimes\ket{\Omega}^{\alpha})\oplus V|_{\mathcal{H}^{X,<r}}
and
V_{2}=(\ket{\Omega,\geq r}\bra{\Omega,\geq r}^{X}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{\alpha})\oplus V|_{\mathcal{H}^{X,\geq r}}
. Note that the image of
V_{2}
lies in
(\mathcal{H}^{Y,\geq r}\oplus\ket{\Omega,\geq r}^{Y})\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
due to
Eq.

97
. These are isometries as they are direct sums of isometries with orthogonal images.

Hence, we have

\text{tr}_{Y,\geq r}\text{tr}_{\alpha}V=(\text{tr}V_{2})\circ\text{tr}_{Y,\geq r}V_{1}=(\text{tr}_{X,\geq r}\otimes\text{tr}_{\alpha})\circ\text{tr}_{Y,\geq r}V_{1}=\text{tr}_{\alpha}\text{tr}_{Y,\geq r}V_{1}\circ\text{tr}_{X,\geq r}=\text{tr}_{Y,\geq r}\text{tr}_{\alpha}V\circ\text{tr}_{X,\geq r}

(102)

where we used that
V_{2}
is an isometry, i.e., in particular trace-preserving and in the last equality we are allowed to commute
\text{tr}_{X,\geq r}
past
V_{1}
as
V_{1}
has no such output wire to trace out.

We then have

\text{tr}_{>r}\circ\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\text{tr}_{>r}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}_{one}=\text{tr}_{>r}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\text{tr}_{>r}\circ\mathcal{C}_{one}=\text{tr}_{>r}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}_{one}\circ\text{tr}_{>r}.

(103)

where we used that
\mathcal{C}_{one}
is a pseudo-causal box. If
Eq.

100
holds, we can show causality by noting that the image of
V_{2}
lies in
(\mathcal{H}^{Y,>r}\oplus\ket{\Omega,>r}^{Y})\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
which then leads to an analogous equation to
Eq.

102
, namely that
\text{tr}_{Y,>r}\text{tr}_{\alpha}V=(\text{tr}V_{2})\circ\text{tr}_{Y,>r}V_{1}=(\text{tr}_{X,\geq r}\otimes\text{tr}_{\alpha})\circ\text{tr}_{Y,>r}V_{1}=\text{tr}_{\alpha}\text{tr}_{Y,>r}V_{1}\circ\text{tr}_{X,\geq r}=\text{tr}_{Y,>r}\text{tr}_{\alpha}V\circ\text{tr}_{X,\geq r}
.
∎

Lemma 11

(Causality functions remove maximal elements)
.

Let
\chi
be a causality function on a finite spacetime
\mathcal{T}
. For all bottom-closed
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
and all
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
which are maximal in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, it holds that
t\not\in\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We can write
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\{t\}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\preceq t}
where
\mathcal{T}^{\preceq t}=\{t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}|t^{\prime}\preceq t\}
(which is bottom-closed by definition). The set
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\{t\}
is also bottom-closed as
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is bottom-closed and
t
is maximal in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. Then, since
\chi
respects unions of bottom-closed sets

\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})=\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\{t\})\cup\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\preceq t})

(104)

We have that
t\not\in\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\{t\})\subsetneq\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\{t\}
. But we also have that
\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\preceq t})\subsetneq\mathcal{T}^{\preceq t}
is bottom-closed but
\mathcal{T}^{\preceq t}
is the smallest bottom-closed set that contains
t
. Hence,
t\not\in\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\preceq t})
and thus
t\not\in\chi(\mathcal{T}^{\prime})
.
∎

Lemma 12

(Certain implies non-disturbing)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{XY,\mathcal{T}})
be a normalised or subnormalised causal box and
\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{R,T})
a normalised pseudo-causal box such that

\mathcal{P}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}_{n}\otimes\ket{n,T}\bra{n,T}^{R}

(105)

where
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is a projector for each
n
.
If

(\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{n})^{\hookrightarrow}=0

(106)

for all
n\neq 0
, then

(\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{0})^{\hookrightarrow}=\mathcal{C}^{\hookrightarrow}.

(107)

Proof.

Consider a purification of
\mathcal{C}
,
V:\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{XY,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\alpha,T}
where
T\succ t
for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
. This can be chosen to be a causal box due to
Lemma

8
for any choice of finite-dimensional
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
. For
n\neq 0
, we have (writing
\mathcal{P}_{n}=P_{n}\cdot P_{n}
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&=(\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{n})^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\text{tr}_{\alpha}V\circ\mathcal{P}_{n})^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=\text{tr}_{\alpha}\circ(VP_{n}\cdot P_{n}V^{\dagger})^{\hookrightarrow}\end{aligned}

(108)

where we used associativity of the link product in the last line.

As
\text{tr}_{\alpha}
is completely positive this implies that
(VP_{n}\cdot P_{n}V^{\dagger})^{\hookrightarrow}=0
. Calculating the Choi-Jamiołkowski representation of the latter, we find for any
\ket{\psi},\ket{\varphi}\in\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\alpha,T}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{R,T}
and any choice of basis
\ket{i}
of
\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&=\sum_{ij}\bra{\psi}\bra{i}VP_{n}\ket{i}\bra{j}P_{n}V^{\dagger}\ket{j}\ket{\varphi}\\
&=\sum_{i}\bra{\psi}\bra{i}VP_{n}\ket{i}\sum_{j}\bra{j}P_{n}V^{\dagger}\ket{j}\ket{\varphi}.\end{aligned}

(109)

This can only be true if
\sum_{i}\bra{\psi}\bra{i}VP_{n}\ket{i}=0
for all
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{F}^{Y,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\alpha,T}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{R,T}
. We then find using that
\sum_{n}P_{n}
is a decomposition of unity (this follows from the fact that
\mathcal{P}
is normalised)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R_{V^{\hookrightarrow}}(\ket{\psi},\ket{\varphi})&=\sum_{ij}\bra{\psi}\bra{i}V\ket{i}\bra{j}V^{\dagger}\ket{j}\ket{\varphi}\\
&=\sum_{ij}\sum_{nm}\bra{\psi}\bra{i}VP_{n}\ket{i}\bra{j}P_{m}V^{\dagger}\ket{j}\ket{\varphi}\\
&=\sum_{nm}\sum_{ij}\bra{\psi}\bra{i}VP_{n}\ket{i}\bra{j}P_{m}V^{\dagger}\ket{j}\ket{\varphi}\\
&=\sum_{ij}\bra{\psi}\bra{i}VP_{0}\ket{i}\bra{j}P_{0}V^{\dagger}\ket{j}\ket{\varphi}\\
&=R_{(V\circ P_{0})^{\hookrightarrow}}(\ket{\psi},\ket{\varphi}).\end{aligned}

(110)

Here, we used that the sum converges absolutely and to the same value for any choice of basis. Hence, we can choose the basis to be one such that
P_{n}\ket{i}\in\{0,1\}
in which case the exchange of sums corresponds simply to a different order of summation which is allowed due to absolute convergence.

We thus find
V^{\hookrightarrow}=(V\circ P_{0})^{\hookrightarrow}
and the statement follows after tracing out the purifying system.
∎

Corollary 5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{XY,\mathcal{T}})
be a normalised causal box and
\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{X,\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{R,T})
a normalised pseudo-causal box such that

\mathcal{P}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}_{n}\otimes\ket{n,T}\bra{n,T}^{R}

(111)

where
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is a projector for each
n
. If

\text{tr}((\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{0})^{\hookrightarrow})=1

(112)

then

(\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{0})^{\hookrightarrow}=\mathcal{C}^{\hookrightarrow}.

(113)

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{P}
is normalised, we have that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 1&=\text{tr}((\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P})^{\hookrightarrow})\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\text{tr}((\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{n})^{\hookrightarrow})\\
&=\text{tr}((\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{0})^{\hookrightarrow})+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{tr}((\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{n})^{\hookrightarrow})\\
&=1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{tr}((\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{n})^{\hookrightarrow})\end{aligned}

(114)

Since
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is a subnormalised pseudo-causal box,
\text{tr}((\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{n})^{\hookrightarrow})
is non-negative. Thus, the above equation implies that

\text{tr}((\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{n})^{\hookrightarrow})=0

(115)

for
n\neq 0
which is only possible if

(\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{n})^{\hookrightarrow}=0

(116)

as
(\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{P}_{n})^{\hookrightarrow}
must be a (subnormalised) state. Hence, the conditions of
Lemma

12
are satisfied and we arrive at the conclusion.
∎

A.2
Useful properties of process box protocols

Corollary 6

(Normalisation condition for AO)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}=(\mathcal{C},(\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
be a causal box protocol. Then, it satisfies AO if for all
k,x_{k}

\text{tr}((\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}(\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}))^{\hookrightarrow})=1.

(117)

Proof.

This follows immediately from
5
. We choose
\mathcal{C}\otimes\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
as the normalised causal box and
\mathcal{V}^{I}_{count}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{O}_{count}
as the normalised pseudo-causal box, where

\mathcal{V}^{I/O}_{count}:=\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{V}^{I/O,\mathcal{T}}_{k,count}

(118)

and
\mathcal{V}^{I/O,\mathcal{T}}_{k,count}=V^{I/O,\mathcal{T}}_{k,count}\cdot V^{I/O,\mathcal{T}\dagger}_{k,count}
(see
Eq.

83
for the definition). The condition in
5
then holds for the outcome
\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}(\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k})
by assumption. Thus, we have that

((\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}(\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}))\circ(\mathcal{C}\otimes\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}))^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}(\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}))^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}.

(119)

Since this holds for all
k,x_{k}
, the causal box protocol satisfies AO.
∎

Lemma 13

(Comb decomposition of LO)
.

The maps

\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}P^{I,t}_{k}\otimes\ket{0,t}^{\alpha}

(120)

and

\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}P^{O,t}_{k}\otimes\bra{0,t}^{\alpha}.

(121)

where
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}=\mathbb{C}
are subnormalised pseudo-causal boxes. From this it follows that
\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})
(related to the above maps via
Eq.

46
) is a subnormalised causal box.

Proof.

The map in
Eq.

120
is equal to

P^{I}_{k}\circ\bigotimes_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}((\mathbb{1}^{A^{I}_{k},t}-\ket{\Omega,t}\bra{\Omega,t}^{A^{I}_{k}})\otimes\ket{0,t}^{\alpha}+\ket{\Omega,t}\bra{\Omega,t}^{A^{I}_{k}}\otimes\ket{\Omega,t}^{\alpha})

(122)

where
\mathbb{1}^{A^{I}_{k},t}
is the identity on
\mathcal{F}^{A^{I}_{k},t}
and
P^{I}_{k}
projects
\mathcal{F}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}
onto its one-message space
\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}
. To see this, notice that
P^{I}_{k}=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}P^{I,t}_{k}
and that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P^{I,t}_{k}&\circ\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}((\mathbb{1}^{A^{I}_{k},t^{\prime}}-\ket{\Omega,t^{\prime}}\bra{\Omega,t^{\prime}}^{A^{I}_{k}})\otimes\ket{0,t^{\prime}}^{\alpha}+\ket{\Omega,t^{\prime}}\bra{\Omega,t^{\prime}}^{A^{I}_{k}}\otimes\ket{\Omega,t^{\prime}}^{\alpha})\\
&=P^{I,t}_{k}\circ((\mathbb{1}^{A^{I}_{k},t}-\ket{\Omega,t}\bra{\Omega,t}^{A^{I}_{k}})\otimes\ket{0,t}^{\alpha}\otimes\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}\neq t}\ket{\Omega,t}\bra{\Omega,t}^{A^{I}_{k}}\otimes\ket{\Omega,t}^{\alpha})\\
&=P^{I,t}_{k}\circ(\mathbb{1}^{A^{I}_{k},t}\otimes\ket{0,t}^{\alpha}\otimes\bigotimes_{t^{\prime}\neq t}\ket{\Omega,t}\bra{\Omega,t}^{A^{I}_{k}}\otimes\ket{\Omega,t}^{\alpha})\\
&=P^{I,t}_{k}\otimes\ket{0,t}^{\alpha}\end{aligned}

(123)

where we used the wire isomorphism in the last equality.

Note that
P^{I}_{k}
is a subnormalised pseudo-causal box due to
Lemma

2
while
\bigotimes_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}((\mathbb{1}^{A^{I}_{k},t}-\ket{\Omega,t}\bra{\Omega,t}^{A^{I}_{k}})\otimes\ket{0,t}^{\alpha}+\ket{\Omega,t}\bra{\Omega,t}^{A^{I}_{k}}\otimes\ket{\Omega,t}^{\alpha})
is a normalised pseudo-causal box, hence, by
Lemma

7
their composition is again a subnormalised pseudo-causal box.

On the other hand,
Eq.

121
is equal to (where
P_{k}^{O}=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}P^{O,t}_{k}
)

\bra{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}^{\alpha\beta}V_{comp,k}\circ P^{O}_{k}

(124)

where
V_{comp,k}
acts locally at each time step
t
as (where
\ket{\psi_{n},t}
is an
n
-message state)

V_{comp,k}(\ket{\psi_{n},t}^{A^{O}_{k}}\bigotimes_{m}\ket{0,t}^{\alpha})=\begin{cases}\ket{\psi_{n},t}^{A^{O}_{k}}\bigotimes_{m-n}\ket{0,t}^{\alpha}\otimes\ket{\Omega,t}^{\beta},&m\geq n\\
\ket{\psi_{n},t}^{A^{O}_{k}}\bigotimes_{n-m}\ket{0,t}^{\alpha}\otimes\ket{0,t}^{\beta},&m<n\end{cases}

(125)

In the above, the zero-fold tensor product on
\alpha
corresponds to the vacuum state
\ket{\Omega,t}^{\alpha}
, hence this state is obtained whenever
m=n
.
This is an isometry and a pseudo-causal box as the output at
t
only depends on the input at
t
. Hence,
\bra{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}^{\alpha\beta}V_{comp,k}
is a subnormalised pseudo-causal box by definition and
Eq.

124
is also a subnormalised pseudo-causal box by
Lemmas

2
and
7
.

To see that
Eqs.

121
and
124
are indeed equal note that
P^{O}_{k}
projects onto the subspace where there is exactly one message on
A^{O}_{k}
anywhere in
\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}
and that
\bra{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}^{\alpha\beta}V_{comp,k}
projects onto the subspace where there is an equal number of messages on
A^{O}_{k}
and on
\alpha
at every time step. Thus, both together project onto the subspace where there is exactly one message on
A^{O}_{k}
and
\alpha
at the same
t
, which is precisely what
Eq.

121
does.

Hence,
\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})
is a subnormalised causal box by
Lemma

7
as the sequential composition of a subnormalised pseudo-causal box, a causal box and then again a subnormalised pseudo-causal box.
∎

See
4

Proof.

Using that
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
satisfies ALO, we immediately find

\mathcal{P}^{O,r}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I,t}_{k}=\mathcal{P}^{O,r}_{k}\circ\mathcal{P}^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I,t}_{k}=\delta_{r,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}\mathcal{P}^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I,t}_{k}=\delta_{r,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I,t}_{k}.

(126)

Let now
V_{k}:\mathcal{F}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{A^{O}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha}
be a purification of
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
. Then, due to the above and the positivity of the trace we have

P^{O,r}_{k}\circ V_{k}\circ P^{I,t}_{k}=\delta_{r,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}V_{k}\circ P^{I,t}_{k}.

(127)

Note that this implies the weaker statement

V_{k}\circ P^{I}_{k}=P^{O}_{k}\circ V_{k}\circ P^{I}_{k}.

(128)

Plugging in
P^{I/O}_{k}=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{I/O}_{k}}P^{I/O,t}_{k}
on the RHS,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} V_{k}\circ P^{I}_{k}&=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k},t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}P^{O,t^{\prime}}_{k}\circ V_{k}\circ P^{I,t}_{k}\\
&=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}P^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}_{k}\circ V_{k}\circ P^{I,t}_{k}\\
&=P_{eff,k}(V_{k})\end{aligned}

(129)

where we used
Eq.

127
. Tracing out the purifying system
\alpha
, we find
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}=\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})
.
∎

Lemma 14

(Process box protocols are behaviourally equivalent to protocols satisfying ALO)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}=(\mathcal{C},(\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
be a process box protocol on a totally ordered spacetime. Then, there exists a process box protocol
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C},(\{\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
which satisfies ALO and is behaviourally equivalent to
\mathfrak{P}
.

Proof.

We define the subnormalised causal box

\mathcal{M}_{x_{k},sub}^{\prime A_{k}}=\mathcal{V}_{LO,k}\circ(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{k})\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{LO,k}.

(130)

where
\mathcal{P}^{I}_{LO,k}
is defined as in
Eq.

120
,
\mathcal{O}_{k}=\sum_{t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}^{\alpha}\bra{t}^{\alpha}\cdot\ket{t^{\prime}}^{\alpha}\bra{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t^{\prime})}^{\alpha}
(note that the maps
\mathcal{O}_{k}:\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}
are specified by the given protocol
\mathfrak{P}
(involved in its LO condition)) and
\mathcal{V}_{LO,k}(\cdot)=V_{LO,k}\cdot V_{LO,k}^{\dagger}
where
V_{LO,k}:\mathcal{F}^{A^{O}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{F}(\ket{0}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{A^{O}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\beta,\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{F}(\ket{0}^{\gamma}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}})
(with
\mathcal{H}^{\beta}\cong\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k}}
) acts separately on each time stamp as (where
\ket{\psi_{n},t}
is an
n
-message state)

V_{LO,k}\ket{\psi_{n},t}^{A^{O}_{k}}\bigotimes_{m}\ket{0,t}^{\alpha}=\begin{cases}\ket{\psi_{n},t}^{A^{O}_{k}}\ket{\Omega,t}^{\beta}\ket{\Omega,t}^{\gamma},&m=n\\
(\bigotimes_{m}\ket{0,t}^{A^{O}_{k}})\ket{\psi_{n},t}^{\beta}\ket{0,t}^{\gamma},&n\neq m.\end{cases}

(131)

This is an isometry and respects pseudo-causality as the output at
t
depends only on the input at
t
. Hence,
V_{LO,k}
is a pseudo-causal box. Thus,
\mathcal{M}_{x_{k},sub}^{\prime A_{k}}
is indeed a subnormalised causal box by
Lemma

7
.

As
\mathcal{M}_{x_{k},sub}^{\prime A_{k}}
is a subnormalised causal box, there exists by definition (
3
) a normalised causal box
\mathcal{M}_{x_{k},norm}^{\prime A_{k}}
such that
\mathcal{M}_{x_{k},sub}^{\prime A_{k}}(\cdot)=\bra{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}^{R}\mathcal{M}_{x_{k},norm}^{\prime A_{k}}(\cdot)\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}^{R}
. We now define

\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}=\text{tr}_{R\beta\gamma}\circ\mathcal{M}_{x_{k},norm}^{\prime A_{k}}

(132)

As
\mathcal{M}_{x_{k},sub}^{\prime A_{k}}
is obtained through sequential composition of a map (
\mathcal{P}^{I}_{LO,k}
) which is CPTP when restricted to the one-message space followed by other CPTP maps, it is also be CPTP when restricted to the one-message space, and so we must have that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}=\text{tr}_{\beta\gamma}\circ\mathcal{M}_{x_{k},sub}^{\prime A_{k}}
for
\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
to be CPTP.

The map
\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
satisfies ALO as
\mathcal{O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{LO,k}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I,t}_{k}=\mathcal{P}^{I,t}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{k}(\ket{0,t}\bra{0,t}^{\alpha})=\mathcal{P}^{I,t}_{k}\otimes\ket{0,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}\bra{0,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}^{\alpha}
and from
Eq.

131
, we see that
V_{LO,k}
maps the subspace
\mathcal{F}^{A^{O}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}\otimes\ket{0,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}^{\alpha}
to
\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k},\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\beta,\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}\otimes(\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}^{\gamma}\oplus\ket{0,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}^{\gamma})
. The projector
\mathcal{P}^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}_{k}
acts trivially on this space.

To check AO and behavioural equivalence, note first that
\bra{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}^{\beta\gamma}P^{O}_{k}\circ V_{LO,k}=P^{O}_{LO,k}
(cf.
Eq.

124
). Thus,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\text{tr}_{\beta\gamma R}&(\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}\bra{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}^{\beta\gamma R}\circ\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}_{x_{k},norm}^{\prime A_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}))^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\text{tr}_{\beta\gamma}(\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}\bra{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}^{\beta\gamma}\circ\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}_{x_{k},sub}^{\prime A_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}))^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}^{O}_{LO,k}\circ(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{k})\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{LO,k})^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}))^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}\end{aligned}

(133)

where we used the decomposition of
\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}
from
Eq.

46
and that
\mathfrak{P}
satisfies LO. As the last line has trace 1, we can drop the projectors
\ket{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}\bra{\Omega,\mathcal{T}}^{\beta\gamma R},\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k},\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}
by
5
, giving us both AO and behavioural equivalence,

(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}_{x_{k}}^{\prime A_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k})^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}

(134)

∎

Lemma 15

(1-message output)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}=(\mathcal{C},(\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
be a process box protocol on a totally ordered spacetime. Then, there exists a causal box
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
such that the process box protocol
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},(\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
is behaviourally equivalent to
\mathfrak{P}
and

\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}\circ\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

(135)

Proof.

We define

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}:=\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N+1}\mathcal{C}^{I}_{one,k}\circ\mathcal{C}

(136)

where
\mathcal{C}^{I}_{one,k}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{F}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}})
is a pseudo-causal box which acts as identity on the one-message space and whose image is the one-message space (the existence of such pseudo-causal boxes is shown in
Lemma

9
). As each
\mathcal{C}^{I}_{one,k}
is a normalised pseudo-causal box,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is a normalised causal box by
Lemma

7
. Further,
Eq.

135
is satisfied as the image of
\mathcal{C}^{I}_{one,k}
is the one-message input space of agent
k
. The resulting protocol
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
is a process box protocol. For this notice that
\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N+1}(\mathcal{C}^{I}_{one,k}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k})\circ\mathcal{C}=\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}\circ\mathcal{C}
as
\mathcal{C}^{I}_{one,k}
is the identity on the one-message space and thus

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N+1}\mathcal{C}_{one,k}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}&=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}\\
(\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N+1}\mathcal{C}_{one,k}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}(\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}))^{\hookrightarrow}&=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}\\
(\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N+1}\mathcal{C}_{one,k}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}))^{\hookrightarrow}&=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}\end{aligned}

(137)

using AO (together with
Lemma

3
) of
\mathfrak{P}
in the first two lines and LO of
\mathfrak{P}
and the fact that
\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}=\mathcal{P}_{eff,k}(\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})
in the last line. As the RHS is a normalised causal box and thus has trace 1, by
5
, the above equalities still hold true if we remove
\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}
on the LHS, yielding respectively behavioural equivalence, AO and LO.
∎

A.3
Proofs of all results

See
5

Proof.

Relabelling the systems which are composed cannot change the result of the composition. Hence,

(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}.

(138)

We now need to show that
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},(\{\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
is in fact a causal box protocol (i.e.,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
for all
k,x_{k}
are causal boxes) and that if
\mathfrak{P}
is a process box protocol, then so is
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.

Let us first check that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
is a causal box. Let
\chi_{k}
be a causality function for
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
. We now define a causality function
\chi^{\prime}_{k}
for
\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
defined on any bottom-closed subsets
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
as

\chi^{\prime}_{k}:\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\mapsto bc(\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}\circ\chi_{k}\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}})))

(139)

where
bc(\cdot)
refers to the bottom-closure of the set in the argument and
\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}))
refers to the preimage of
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}})
under
\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}
. As
\chi_{k}
only acts on bottom-closed sets, we have to show that
\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}})
is bottom-closed for all bottom-closed
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. Let
t_{1}\prec t_{2}
with
t_{2}\in\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}))
, then, due to
Eq.

52
, it follows that
\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}(t_{1})\prec\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}(t_{2})\in\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}})
. As
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}
is a bottom-closed set, we thus have
\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}(t_{1})\in\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}})
and hence
t_{1}\in\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}))
. Thus,
\chi^{\prime}_{k}
is well-defined.

We now check that
\chi^{\prime}_{k}
is a valid causality function. It commutes with taking the union as
bc,\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}},\chi_{k},\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}
all do, hence their composition does as well. It also respects inclusions for the same reason. Finally, it maps any bottom-closed set to a proper subset of itself. As
\tilde{\mathcal{T}},\chi^{\prime}_{k}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}})
are both bottom-closed it suffices to show that for a maximal element
t\in\chi^{\prime}_{k}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}})
, there exists
t^{\prime}\in\tilde{\mathcal{T}}
with
t^{\prime}\succ t
. As
t
is maximal, there exists
t^{\prime\prime}\in\chi_{k}(\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}})))
such that
t=\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}(t^{\prime\prime})
. Hence, there exists
\tilde{t}\in\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}))
with
\tilde{t}\succ t^{\prime\prime}
due to
Lemma

11
applied to the causality function
\chi_{k}
. Due to the first line of
Eq.

52
, it then follows
\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{t})\succ\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}(t^{\prime\prime})=t
and thus
\chi^{\prime}_{k}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}})\subsetneq\tilde{\mathcal{T}}
. Hence,
\chi^{\prime}_{k}
is a valid causality function.

We now check that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
satisfies causality with the causality function
\chi^{\prime}_{k}
. For this, notice first that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\tilde{\mathcal{T}}}\circ\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}&=\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}))}\\
\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}^{-1}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\chi^{\prime}_{k}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}})}&=\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}^{-1}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash bc(\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}\circ\chi_{k}\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}})))}\\
&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}^{-1}(bc(\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}\circ\chi_{k}\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}})))\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}))}\circ\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}^{-1}\\
&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\chi_{k}(\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}})))}\circ\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}^{-1}.\end{aligned}

(140)

Using this, we find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\tilde{\mathcal{T}}}\circ\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\chi(\tilde{\mathcal{T}})}&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\tilde{\mathcal{T}}}\circ\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{I_{k}}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\chi(\tilde{\mathcal{T}})}\\
&=\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}))}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\chi_{k}(\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}})))}\circ\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}^{-1}\\
&=\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{O_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}))}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}^{-1}\\
&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\tilde{\mathcal{T}}}\circ\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}^{-1}\\
&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\tilde{\mathcal{T}}}\circ\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}.\end{aligned}

(141)

where in the third line we used that
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
satisfies causality for
\chi_{k}
.

Let us now check that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is a causal box. Let
\chi
be a causality function for
\mathcal{C}
. We define a causality function
\chi^{\prime}
for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
via

\chi^{\prime}:\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\mapsto bc(\bigcup_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}\circ\chi\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{I_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}})).

(142)

The argument that this is a valid causality functions is analogous to the argument we used for
\chi^{\prime}_{k}
(now using the second line of
Eq.

52
for the proof that
\chi^{\prime}
maps bottom-closed sets to proper subsets of themselves).

We then have analogously as before

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\tilde{\mathcal{T}}}\circ\mathcal{R}&=\mathcal{R}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash(\bigcup_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{I_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}))}\\
\mathcal{R}^{-1}\circ\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\backslash\chi^{\prime}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}})}&=\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}\backslash\chi(\bigcup_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{R}^{-1}_{I_{k}}(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}\cap\text{Im}(\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}})))}\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1}.\end{aligned}

(143)

where we additionally used that
\chi
respects (finite) unions. From this it follows that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
respects causality for
\chi^{\prime}
by an analogous calculation as in
Eq.

141
. Hence,
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
is a causal box protocol.

If
\mathfrak{P}
is a process box protocol, then
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
must satisfy AO and LO (for the relabelled
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{k}(\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}(t))=\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}(\mathcal{O}_{k}(t))
). For this notice that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I/O}_{k}&=\mathcal{P}^{\prime I/O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{R}\\
\mathcal{R}\circ P^{I/O,t}_{k}&=P^{\prime I/O,\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}/O_{k}}(t)}_{k}\circ\mathcal{R}\end{aligned}

(144)

where we added a prime to the projectors on the relabelled Fock spaces to distinguish them from the projectors on the original spaces. Hence, with the definition
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{k}(\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}(t))=\mathcal{R}_{{O}_{k}}(\mathcal{O}_{k}(t))
and using again that relabelling composed systems does not change the overall composition, we find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}^{\prime O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{\prime I}_{k})^{\hookrightarrow}&=(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}^{\prime O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{P}^{\prime I}_{k})^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}^{O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1})^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}\end{aligned}

(145)

where we used that
\mathcal{R}
is a unitary and that
\mathfrak{P}
satisfies AO in the second to last line and behavioural equivalence between the two protocols in the last line. Thus, the new protocol satisfies AO. Further, we find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\sum_{t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}P^{\prime O,\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{k}(\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}(t))}_{k}(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1}(P^{\prime I,\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}(t)}_{k}\cdot P^{\prime I,\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}(t^{\prime})}_{k}))P^{\prime O,\mathcal{O}^{\prime}_{k}(\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}(t^{\prime}))}_{k})^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{R}^{-1}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\sum_{t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}\mathcal{R}\circ(P^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}_{k}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}(P^{I,t}_{k}\mathcal{R}^{-1}(\cdot)P^{I,t^{\prime}}_{k})P^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t^{\prime})}_{k}))^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}\end{aligned}

(146)

where again we used that
\mathcal{R}
is a unitary and that
\mathfrak{P}
satisfies LO in the second to last line and behavioural equivalence between the two protocols in the last line. Thus, (since
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
also already satisfies AO) the new protocol satisfies LO. Thus, it is a process box protocol.
∎

See
1

Proof.

We will prove the theorem iteratively by showing that if a process box protocol satisfies the first
n=0,1,2,3,4
properties, then there exists a behaviourally equivalent process box protocol which satisfies the first
n+1
properties.

Property 1:
Let
\mathfrak{P}=(\mathcal{C},(\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
be an arbitrary process box protocol on a spacetime
\mathcal{T}
. Let
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}=\{1,...,M\}
with
M=|\mathcal{T}|
. Let
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{T^{\prime}}
be an isomorphic map such that for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
with
t_{1}\prec t_{2}
it holds that
\mathcal{R}(t_{1})\prec\mathcal{R}(t_{2})
(it is always possible to find such a map by the order-extension theorem
[
69
]
). By
Lemma

5
, the protocol
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
which is obtained from
\mathfrak{P}
as in
Lemma

5
via the relabelling
\mathcal{R}
is then behaviourally equivalent to
\mathfrak{P}
and also a process box protocol.

Properties 2&3:
This is shown in
Lemma

16
.

Property 4:
According to
Lemma

15
, we can always find a behaviourally equivalent process box protocol where property 4 holds. As the agent operations and the spacetime remain the same, properties 1, 2 and 3 still hold.

Property 5:
This follows from
Lemma

5
with the relabeling (where
k=1,...,N
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{R}_{P}(1)=1\\
&\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}(m)=2(m-1)N+2k\\
&\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}(m)=2(m-2)N+2k+1\\
&\mathcal{R}_{F}(M)=2(M-1)N+2.\end{aligned}

(147)

This satisfies the condition of
Lemma

5
as
\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}},\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}
are obviously strictly order-preserving and for any
k,l
and
m

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}(m)&=2(m-1)N+2k<2(m-1)N+2k+1=\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}(m+1)\\
\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}(m)&=2(m-2)N+2k+1<2mN+2l=\mathcal{R}_{I_{l}}(m+1)\end{aligned}

(148)

where in the bottom line we used that
2k\leq 2N
and
l\geq 1
. Note that we can choose
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
to be the union of images of
\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}/O_{k}}
together with time stamps for the global past, future and the result wire so that every time stamp
t\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is indeed an element of some
\mathcal{T}^{\prime I/O}_{k}
.
Further, properties 1, 2, 3 and 4 of
1
still apply. The new spacetime is still totally ordered, the global past is still the only agent with trivial input and we have that
\mathcal{R}_{I_{k}}(m)=2(m-1)N+2k
while
\mathcal{R}_{O_{k}}(m+1)=2(m-1)N+2k+1
. Thus,
\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)=t+1
and a relabelling does not affect the form of the agent operations. A relabelling does not change the number of messages, hence, the image of the causal box is still the 1-message space. Finally, property 5 is satisfied as can be readily seen from the definition of
\mathcal{R}
. For any agent the (relabelled) input time stamps are manifestly even, while the relabelled output time stamps are manifestly odd (which also implies there can be no overlap between input and output time stamps). Further, if
t
is an input time stamp for two agents
k\neq l
, then
t=2(m-1)N+2k=2(m^{\prime}-1)N+2l
for some
m\neq m^{\prime}
, which implies
N(m-m^{\prime})=l-k
and thus
|l-k|\geq N
. However,
0<l,k\leq N
and so this cannot hold. An analogous argument applies to the output time stamps.
∎

Lemma 16

(Time-independent agent operations)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}=(\mathcal{C},(\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
be a process box protocol on a totally ordered spacetime
\mathcal{T}
. Then, there exists a process box protocol
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},(\{\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
which is behaviourally equivalent to
\mathfrak{P}
which satisfies properties 1, 2 and 3 of
1
.

Proof.

W.l.o.g., we assume that the first
n
agents’ input is trivial and the inputs of agents
n+1
to
N
are non-trivial.

By
Lemma

14
, we can assume that
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
, for
k>n
, satisfies ALO (see
9
). Since
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
, and thus in particular its restriction to the one-message space, is a CPTP map, we can write a Kraus decomposition

\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}})}=\sum_{i}A^{i}_{k,x_{k}}\cdot A^{i\dagger}_{k,x_{k}}.

(149)

As
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
satisfies ALO, we must have that
P^{O,r}_{k}\circ A^{i}_{k,x_{k}}\circ P^{I,t}_{k}=0
if
r\neq\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)
if the agent
k
has non-trivial input. Hence, we can write

\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}})}=\sum_{i}\sum_{t,t^{\prime}}A^{i,t}_{k,x_{k}}\cdot A^{i,t^{\prime}\dagger}_{k,x_{k}}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}\bra{t}\cdot\ket{t^{\prime}}\bra{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t^{\prime})}

(150)

where
A^{i,t}_{k,x_{k}}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}\bra{t}=A^{i}_{k,x_{k}}\circ P^{I,t}
(
=P^{O,\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}\circ A^{i}_{k,x_{k}}\circ P^{I,t}
due to ALO) if
A_{k}
has non-trivial input. If
A_{k}
has trivial input, then
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
must be a state in the 1-message space, as otherwise AO could not be satisfied, and we can write (note that the Kraus operators are states in the Hilbert space here)

\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}=\sum_{i}\sum_{t,t^{\prime}}A^{i,t}_{k,x_{k}}A^{i,t^{\prime}\dagger}_{k,x_{k}}\otimes\ket{t}\bra{t^{\prime}}.

(151)

We define now if
A_{k}
has non-trivial input

\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime I}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}})}=\sum_{i}\sum_{t,t^{\prime},r,r^{\prime}}A^{i,r}_{k,x_{k}}\cdot A^{i,r^{\prime}\dagger}_{k,x_{k}}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(r)}\bra{r}\cdot\ket{r^{\prime}}\bra{\mathcal{O}_{k}(r^{\prime})}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\ket{t+1}\bra{t}\cdot\ket{t^{\prime}}\bra{t^{\prime}+1}

(152)

where the system
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}
is considered to be part of the message (one can think of
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}
as storing when the message should have been sent, or equivalently when it should be processed by the causal box, in the original protocol). Clearly, this is time-independent as we can write (somewhat abusing notation)

\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime I}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}})}=\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}})}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{+1}

(153)

where
\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}})}
acts only on the message part (i.e.,
A^{I}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}
) of the state and
\mathcal{T}_{+1}
acts only on the time stamp part of the state (increasing it by one). This can be extended to a causal box on the full Fock space due to
Lemma

10
.

On the other hand, if
A_{k}
has trivial input, we define

\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}=\sum_{i}\sum_{r,r^{\prime}}A^{i,r}_{k,x_{k}}A^{i,r^{\prime}\dagger}_{k,x_{k}}\otimes\ket{r}\bra{r^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\ket{t=1}\bra{t=1}

(154)

We further define the causal box
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}(\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N}\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}))\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N}\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}))

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\bigotimes_{k=n+1}^{N}\mathcal{COPY}_{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k},k}\circ(\mathcal{C}\otimes\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\beta})\circ\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N}\mathcal{V}_{diff,k}

(155)

where
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}=\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N}\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}
,

\mathcal{COPY}_{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k},k}|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}})}=\sum_{ss^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k}}\mathcal{I}^{A^{I}_{k}}\otimes\ket{s}\bra{s^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\ket{s}\bra{s}\cdot\ket{s^{\prime}}\bra{s^{\prime}}

(156)

coherently copies the time stamp after
\mathcal{C}
acts onto
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{V}_{diff,k}=V_{diff,k}\cdot V_{diff,k}^{\dagger}
with

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} V_{diff,k}&|_{\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k},\mathcal{T}^{O}_{k}}}=\\
&=\begin{cases}\sum_{t\leq r}\ket{0}^{\beta}\otimes\ket{t-1}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\bra{r}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\ket{r}\bra{t}+\sum_{t>r}\ket{1}^{\beta}\otimes\ket{r}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\bra{r}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\ket{t}\bra{t},&k\leq n\\
\sum_{t\leq r}\ket{0}^{\beta}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t-1)-r}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\bra{r}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\ket{r}\bra{t}+\sum_{t>r}\ket{1}^{\beta}\otimes\ket{r}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\bra{r}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\ket{t}\bra{t},&k>n\end{cases}\end{aligned}

(157)

where
n
is the number of agents with trivial input (this map essentially swaps the time stamp and
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}
and updates the value stored in
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}
according to the function
t\mapsto t-1
for agents with trivial input or
t\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{k}(t-1)-r
for agents with non-trivial output. In this way,
\mathcal{C}
takes the correct time information into account while the updated value on
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}
encodes whether the values of
t,r
match based on what the LO condition of the initial protocol would tell us. The second term is purely to define the map on the entire 1-message space and can be ignored otherwise). This is an isometry as it preserves orthonormality of the basis given by the state
\ket{r}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\ket{t}
. If
k\leq n
, it maps these states to
\ket{0}^{\beta}\ket{t-1}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\ket{r}
or
\ket{1}^{\beta}\ket{r}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\ket{t}
. If
k>n
, they get mapped
\ket{0}^{\beta}\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t-1)-r}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\ket{r}
(orthogonality follows from injectivity of
\mathcal{O}_{k}
) or
\ket{1}^{\beta}\ket{r}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\ket{t}
.

The map
\mathcal{COPY}_{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k},k}
does not change the time stamp of a single message while the map
\mathcal{V}_{diff,k}
either does not change it or increases it. Hence, we can extend them to full pseudo-causal boxes using
Lemma

10
. By
Lemma

7
,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is then a causal box.

This new causal box protocol satisfies ALO w.r.t.
\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)=t+1
for all agents with non-trivial input as all agent operations output a single message at
t+1
when inputting a message at
t
due to
Eq.

153
. It also satisfies AO as, due to being extended using
Lemma

10
, the images of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
as well as of
\mathcal{COPY}_{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k},k}
are the one-message space of the respective agent, i.e.,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}=\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime I}_{k}\circ\mathcal{COPY}_{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k},k}=\mathcal{COPY}_{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k},k}
(where we added primes on the projectors on the Fock spaces of the new protocol to distinguish them from the projectors on the Fock spaces of the original protocol). Hence,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}^{\prime O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{\prime I}_{k})^{\hookrightarrow}&=(\bigotimes_{l=n+1}^{N}\mathcal{P}^{\prime I}_{l}\circ\mathcal{COPY}_{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{l},l}\circ(\mathcal{C}\otimes\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\beta})\circ\bigotimes_{l^{\prime}=1}^{N}\mathcal{V}_{diff,l^{\prime}}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{P}^{\prime O}_{k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}\end{aligned}

(158)

where we used cyclicity of loop composition.

To check behavioural equivalence with
\mathfrak{P}
let us first calculate

\mathcal{V}_{diff,k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{COPY}_{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k},k}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}

(159)

For agents with non-trivial input, we find (using again that both
\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}
and
\mathcal{COPY}_{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k},k}
output to the 1-message space)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{V}_{diff,k}&\circ\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{COPY}_{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{k},k}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}\\
=&\mathcal{V}_{diff,k}\circ\sum_{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}tt^{\prime}rr^{\prime}\\
ss^{\prime}\end{subarray}}A^{i,r}_{k,x_{k}}\cdot A^{i,r^{\prime}\dagger}_{k,x_{k}}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(r)}\braket{r|s}\braket{s^{\prime}|r^{\prime}}\bra{\mathcal{O}_{k}(r^{\prime})}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\ket{t+1}\braket{t|s}\bra{s}\cdot\ket{s^{\prime}}\braket{s^{\prime}|t^{\prime}}\bra{t^{\prime}+1}\\
=&\mathcal{V}_{diff,k}\circ\sum_{i}\sum_{tt^{\prime}}A^{i,t}_{k,x_{k}}\cdot A^{i,t^{\prime}\dagger}_{k,x_{k}}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}\bra{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t^{\prime})}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\ket{t+1}\bra{t}\cdot\ket{t^{\prime}}\bra{t^{\prime}+1}\\
=&\sum_{i}\sum_{tt^{\prime}}A^{i,t}_{k,x_{k}}\cdot A^{i,t^{\prime}\dagger}_{k,x_{k}}\otimes\ket{0}\bra{0}^{\beta}\otimes\ket{0}\bra{0}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}\bra{t}\cdot\ket{t^{\prime}}\bra{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t^{\prime})}\\
=&\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k}\otimes\ket{0}\bra{0}^{\beta}\otimes\ket{0}\bra{0}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\end{aligned}

(160)

where we used that by definition
V_{diff,k}\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\ket{t}=\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)-\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\ket{\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)}
for agents with non-trivial input.

If the input of
A_{k}
is trivial, we find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{V}_{diff,k}\circ\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}}&=\sum_{i}\sum_{r,r^{\prime}}A^{i,r}_{k,x_{k}}A^{i,r^{\prime}\dagger}_{k,x_{k}}\otimes\ket{0}\bra{0}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\otimes\ket{r}\bra{r^{\prime}}\\
&=\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\otimes\ket{0}\bra{0}^{\beta}\otimes\ket{0}\bra{0}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\end{aligned}

(161)

where we used that
V_{diff,k}\ket{r}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\ket{t=1}=\ket{0}^{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,k}}\ket{t=r}
for agents with trivial input. Loop composing this with
\mathcal{C}\otimes\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\beta}
(using associativity of loop composition), we find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\bigotimes_{l=n+1}^{N}\mathcal{COPY}_{\mathcal{T}^{I}_{l},l}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{l}\circ(\mathcal{C}\otimes\text{tr}_{\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\beta})\circ\bigotimes_{l^{\prime}=1}^{N}\mathcal{V}_{diff,l^{\prime}}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}&=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{I}_{k})^{\hookrightarrow}\\
&=(\mathcal{C}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}\end{aligned}

(162)

where we used AO (in the form of
6
) of
\mathfrak{P}
to obtain the second line. As the second line has trace 1 we can drop the 1-message space projectors on the LHS by
5
. Thus, we obtain behavioural equivalence

(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\bigotimes_{k=0}^{N+1}\mathcal{M}^{\prime A_{k}}_{x_{k}})^{\hookrightarrow}

(163)

In order to get rid of agents with trivial input, we first consider a relabelling
\mathcal{R}_{S}(t)=t+1
for all systems
S\neq P
and
\mathcal{R}_{P}(t)=t
. Note that in the latter case
t=1
, and from this it follows straightforwardly that this is a relabelling in accordance with
Lemma

5
. Agents with trivial input which are not the global past thus have operations of the form
\rho_{x_{k}}\otimes\ket{t=2}\bra{t=2}
, where
\rho_{x_{k}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{R_{k}^{x_{k}},T})
in this relabelled protocol which follows directly from applying the relabelling to
Eq.

154
. Replace each such operation with the map which maps an
m
-message it receives at
t
to
\bigodot_{m}\rho
(with
\bigodot_{m=0}\rho_{x_{k}}
being the vacuum as always) at
t+1
(this evidently satisfies ALO with
\mathcal{O}_{k}(t)=t+1
. Thus, the agents
1,...,n
no longer have trivial input. Replace the causal box
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\otimes\bigotimes_{k=1}^{n}\ket{0,t=1}\bra{0,t=1}^{A^{I}_{k}}
. It is immediate that this is still a process box protocol and is behaviourally equivalent to the one we constructed previously.
∎

See
6

Proof.

Throughout this proof, for a linear map
V:\mathcal{H}^{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{BC}
, we will use the notation (note that the states
\ket{\psi_{C}}
in the below can be subnormalised)

\text{Im}_{C}(V):=\text{span}\{\ket{\psi_{C}}\in\mathcal{H}^{C}|\exists\ket{\psi_{A}}\in\mathcal{H}^{A},\ket{\psi_{B}}\in\mathcal{H}^{B}:\ket{\psi_{C}}=\bra{\psi_{B}}V\ket{\psi_{A}}\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{C}.

(164)

We remark that
\text{Im}(V)\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{B}\otimes\text{Im}_{C}(V)
.

Let
V_{1},...,V_{M+1}
be a sequence representation of
\mathcal{C}
such that
V_{m+1}
takes inputs during time step
t=2m+1
and produces outputs during time step
t=2m+2
. Denote with
V:=V_{M+1}\circ...\circ V_{1}
the resulting purification of
\mathcal{C}
.

Due to
Lemma

15
, we can restrict the co-domain of
V_{m+1}

V_{m+1}:\mathcal{F}^{A^{O}_{O(m)},t=2m+1}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{m}}\rightarrow(\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{I(m)},t=2m+2}\oplus\ket{\Omega,t=2m+2}^{A^{I}_{I(m)}})\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{m+1}}

(165)

where
\beta_{m}
denotes an ancilla. W.l.o.g., we assume that
\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{m+1}}=\text{Im}_{\beta_{m+1}}(V_{m+1})
. If this is not the case, replace
\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{m+1}}
in the above equation with
\text{Im}_{\beta_{m+1}}(V_{m+1})
.

We now claim that for each
m
, there exists an ancillary space
\alpha_{m}
and an isometry

\mathcal{E}_{m}:\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{m}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{m}}\otimes\bigoplus_{\mathcal{K}:|\mathcal{K}|\leq m}\ket{\mathcal{K}}

(166)

which can be written as

\mathcal{E}_{m}=\bigoplus_{\mathcal{K}:|\mathcal{K}|\leq m}\mathcal{E}^{\mathcal{K}_{m}}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{K}}

(167)

where the map
\mathcal{E}^{\mathcal{K}_{m}}
acts on the subspace

\text{Im}_{\beta_{m}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m}_{\mathcal{K}}\circ V_{\leq m})

(168)

where
V_{\leq m}:=V_{m}\circ…\circ V_{1}
and
P^{I,t\leq 2m}_{\mathcal{K}}
is the projector onto the subspace

\bigotimes_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},t\leq 2m}\subsetneq\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N+1}\mathcal{F}^{A^{I}_{k},t\leq 2m},

(169)

and maps
\bar{V}_{1},...,\bar{V}_{M+1}
which satisfy
Eq.

61
and (where
m\neq 1
)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bar{V}_{1}&=\mathcal{E}_{1}\circ V_{1}\\
\bar{V}_{m+1}\circ\mathcal{E}_{m}&=\mathcal{E}_{m+1}\circ V_{m+1}\end{aligned}

(170)

The latter implies that
\bar{V}_{1},...,\bar{V}_{M+1}
is a sequence representation of
\mathcal{C}
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bar{V}_{M+1}\circ...\circ\bar{V}_{1}&=\bar{V}_{M+1}\circ...\circ\bar{V}_{2}\circ\mathcal{E}_{1}\circ V_{1}\\
&=\bar{V}_{M+1}\circ...\circ\bar{V}_{3}\circ\mathcal{E}_{2}\circ V_{2}\circ V_{1}\\
&=\mathcal{E}_{M+1}\circ V_{M+1}\circ...\circ V_{1}\\
&=\mathcal{E}_{M+1}\circ V\end{aligned}

(171)

where we repeatedly used
Eq.

170
. As
\mathcal{E}_{M+1}
acts only on the ancilla of the output of
V
and is an isometry, the map
\bar{V}:=\mathcal{E}_{M+1}\circ V
is still a purification of
\mathcal{C}
.

We prove the above claim by induction over
m
.

Consider
V_{1}
and the spaces
\text{Im}_{\beta_{1}}(P^{I,t=2}_{I(0)}\circ V_{1})
and
\text{Im}_{\beta_{1}}(P^{I,\Omega,t=2}_{I(0)}\circ V_{1})
, where
P^{I,\Omega,t=2}_{I(0)}
is the pure projector on the vacuum on the wire
A^{I}_{I(0)}
at
t=2
. We claim that these two spaces are orthogonal. To see this, notice first that

\text{Im}(V)\subseteq\bigotimes_{k}\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},\mathcal{T}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{F}}

(172)

which is simply the purified version of property 4 of
1
. Hence, if we project onto the zero or one-message space at
t=2
, we find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{Im}(V_{\geq 2}\circ P^{I,t=2}_{I(0)}\circ V_{1})&\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{I(0)},t=2}\otimes\bigotimes_{k\neq I(0)}\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},t>2}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{F}}\\
\text{Im}(V_{\geq 2}\circ P^{I,\Omega,t=2}_{I(0)}\circ V_{1})&\subseteq\ket{\Omega,t=2}^{A^{I}_{I(0)}}\otimes\bigotimes_{k}\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},t>2}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{F}}.\end{aligned}

(173)

This implies that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{Im}(V_{\geq 2}|_{\text{Im}_{\beta_{1}}(P^{I,t=2}_{I(0)}\circ V_{1})})&\subseteq\bigotimes_{k\neq I(0)}\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},t>2}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{F}}\\
\text{Im}(V_{\geq 2}|_{\text{Im}_{\beta_{1}}(P^{I,\Omega,t=2}_{I(0)}\circ V_{1})})&\subseteq\bigotimes_{k}\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},t>2}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{F}}.\end{aligned}

(174)

These are orthogonal subspaces as (using the wire isomorphisms) in the first line the space associated to the agent
I(0)
is vacuum at all times and in the second line it is the 1-message space. Hence,
V_{\geq 2}
, an isometry, maps
\text{Im}_{\beta_{1}}(P^{I,t=2}_{I(0)}\circ V_{1})
and
\text{Im}_{\beta_{1}}(P^{I,\Omega,t=2}_{I(0)}\circ V_{1})
to orthogonal subspaces.
This is only possible if these two spaces are themselves orthogonal.

Thus, there exists an isometry

\mathcal{E}_{1}:\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}}\otimes(\ket{\emptyset}\oplus\ket{\{I(0)\}})

(175)

for some large enough
\alpha_{1}
with

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{E}_{1}|_{\text{Im}_{\beta_{1}}(P^{I,t=2}_{I(0)}\circ V_{1})}&=\mathcal{E}^{I(0)}_{1}\otimes\ket{\{I(0)\}}\\
\mathcal{E}_{1}|_{\text{Im}_{\beta_{1}}(P^{I,\Omega,t=2}_{I(0)}\circ V_{1})}&=\mathcal{E}^{\emptyset}_{1}\otimes\ket{\emptyset},\end{aligned}

(176)

where
\mathcal{E}^{I(0)}_{1},\mathcal{E}^{\emptyset}_{1}:\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}}
are arbitrary isometries. Hence,
\mathcal{E}_{1}
satisfies
Eq.

167
by definition.

We define

\bar{V}_{1}:=\mathcal{E}_{1}\circ V_{1}

(177)

which then acts as set out in
Eq.

61
by construction.

Assume now that we have defined the isometries
\mathcal{E}_{m},\bar{V}_{m}
up to some
m
. We now aim to find
\mathcal{E}_{m+1}
.

We proceed analogously to the base case
m=1
.
Let
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
such that
|\mathcal{K}|\leq m
. Using again
Eq.

172
, we find that

\text{Im}(V_{>(m+1)}\circ P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}}\circ V_{\leq(m+1)})\subseteq\bigotimes_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{k},\leq t=2m+2}\otimes\bigotimes_{l\not\in\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{l},t>2m+2}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{F}}

(178)

which then implies that

\text{Im}(V_{>(m+1)}|_{\text{Im}_{\beta_{m+1}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}}\circ V_{\leq(m+1)})})\subseteq\bigotimes_{l\not\in\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{l},t>2m+2}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{F}}.

(179)

Hence,
V_{>(m+1)}
, an isometry, maps
\text{Im}_{\beta_{m}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}}\circ V_{\leq m})
to orthogonal subspaces for different
\mathcal{K}
(note that even a difference in one index makes the spaces for two sets
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
in the above equation orthogonal). This is only possible if these spaces are themselves orthogonal. Note further that since
\bigoplus_{\mathcal{K}}P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}}
acts as identity on
\text{Im}(V_{\leq(m+1)})
, we find that

\bigoplus_{\mathcal{K}}\text{Im}_{\beta_{m+1}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}}\circ V_{\leq(m+1)})=\text{Im}_{\beta_{m+1}}(V_{\leq(m+1)})=\mathcal{H}^{\beta_{m+1}}.

(180)

We thus define
\mathcal{E}_{m+1}
by its action on these orthogonal subspaces,

\mathcal{E}_{m+1}|_{\text{Im}_{\beta_{m}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}}\circ V_{\leq(m+1)})}=\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\mathcal{K}}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{K}}

(181)

for some arbitrary isometries

\mathcal{E}^{\mathcal{K}}_{m+1}:\text{Im}_{\beta_{m}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}}\circ V_{\leq(m+1)})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{m+1}}

(182)

and a large enough space
\alpha_{m+1}
. Thus, we have found an isometry
\mathcal{E}_{m+1}
which satisfies the decomposition
Eq.

167
. We now define (time stamps in the below are implicit)

\bar{V}_{m+1}\ket{\psi}^{A^{O}_{O_{m}}}\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}}:=\begin{cases}\mathcal{E}_{m+1}\circ V_{m+1}\circ\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{m},&\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}}\in\text{Im}(\mathcal{E}_{m})\\
\ket{\Omega}^{A^{I}_{I(m)}}\ket{\psi}^{\alpha_{m+1}^{\prime}}\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{m+1}}\ket{\mathcal{K}},&\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}}\in\text{Im}(\mathcal{E}_{m})^{\perp},I(m)\in\mathcal{K}\\
\ket{0}^{A^{I}_{I(m)}}\ket{\psi}^{\alpha_{m+1}^{\prime}}\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{m+1}}\ket{\mathcal{K}\cup I(m)},&\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}}\in\text{Im}(\mathcal{E}_{m})^{\perp},I(m)\not\in\mathcal{K}\end{cases}

(183)

where
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{m+1}}\cong\mathcal{F}^{A^{O}_{O(m)},t=2m+1},\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{m+1}}\cong\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{m}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{m+1}\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{m+1}}
is some subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{m+1}}
which is orthogonal to
\text{Im}_{\alpha_{m+1}}(\mathcal{E}_{m+1})
(note that we can always assume such a subspace to exist w.l.o.g., as otherwise we can simply consider a larger ancilla).

By definition, we then have the desired relation between
\bar{V}_{m+1},V_{m+1}

\bar{V}_{m+1}\circ\mathcal{E}_{m}=\mathcal{E}_{m+1}\circ V_{m+1}\circ\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{m}\circ\mathcal{E}_{m}=\mathcal{E}_{m+1}\circ V_{m+1}.

(184)

The map
\bar{V}_{m+1}
is an isometry as its restriction to each of the three cases in the definition is clearly an isometry (in each case, it is defined as a composition of isometries). As these cases correspond to orthogonal subspaces and are mapped to orthogonal subspaces the overall map
\bar{V}_{m+1}
is also an isometry.

Further, it satisfies the property in
Eq.

61
. This is obvious in the bottom two cases of the definition. For the top case, let
\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}}\in\text{Im}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
(note that the image of
\mathcal{E}_{m}
is spanned by such product states due to
Eq.

167
which holds by induction assumption). We need to show that
\bar{V}_{m+1}
maps the control to
\ket{\mathcal{K}}
if it outputs vacuum on the agent’s wire and to
\ket{\mathcal{K}\cup I(m)}
if it outputs non-vacuum on the agent’s wire. Using
Eqs.

167
and
168
, we can assume that

\mathcal{E}_{m}^{-1}\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}}\in\text{Im}_{\beta_{m}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m}_{\mathcal{K}}\circ V_{\leq m}).

(185)

From this, it immediately follows that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} P^{I,t=2m+2}_{I(m)}&\circ V_{m+1}\circ\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{m}\ket{\psi}^{A^{O}_{O_{m}}}\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}}\in\text{Im}_{A^{I}_{I(m)}\beta_{m+1}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}\cup I(m)}\circ V_{\leq(m+1)})\\
P^{I,\Omega,t=2m+2}_{I(m)}&\circ V_{m+1}\circ\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{m}\ket{\psi}^{A^{O}_{O_{m}}}\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}}\in\text{Im}_{A^{I}_{I(m)}\beta_{m+1}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}}\circ V_{\leq(m+1)}).\end{aligned}

(186)

The statement then follows from the fact that by definition
\mathcal{E}_{m+1}
maps states in
\text{Im}_{A^{I}_{I(m)}\beta_{m+1}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}\cup I(m)}\circ V_{\leq(m+1)})
to a control
\ket{\mathcal{K}\cup I(m)}
and states in
\text{Im}_{A^{I}_{I(m)}\beta_{m+1}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}}\circ V_{\leq(m+1)})
to a control
\ket{\mathcal{K}}
or more explicitly

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bar{V}_{m+1}\ket{\psi}^{A^{O}_{O_{m}}}\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}}=&\mathcal{E}_{m+1}\circ V_{m+1}\circ\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{m}\ket{\psi}^{A^{O}_{O_{m}}}\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}}\\
=&\mathcal{E}_{m+1}|_{\text{Im}_{\beta_{m+1}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}\cup I(m)}\circ V_{\leq(m+1)})}\circ P^{I,t=2m+2}_{I(m)}\circ V_{m+1}\circ\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{m}\ket{\psi}^{A^{O}_{O_{m}}}\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}}\\
+&\mathcal{E}_{m+1}|_{\text{Im}_{\beta_{m+1}}(P^{I,t\leq 2m+2}_{\mathcal{K}}\circ V_{\leq(m+1)})}\circ P^{I,\Omega,t=2m+2}_{I(m)}\circ V_{m+1}\circ\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{m}\ket{\psi}^{A^{O}_{O_{m}}}\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}}\\
=&(\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\mathcal{K}\cup I(m)}\circ P^{I,t=2m+2}_{I(m)}\circ V_{m+1}\circ\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{m}\ket{\psi}^{A^{O}_{O_{m}}}\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}})\otimes\ket{\mathcal{K}\cup I(m)}\\
+&(\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ P^{I,\Omega,t=2m+2}_{I(m)}\circ V_{m+1}\circ\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{m}\ket{\psi}^{A^{O}_{O_{m}}}\ket{\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha_{m}}\ket{\mathcal{K}})\otimes\ket{\mathcal{K}}\end{aligned}

(187)

where in the second equality we used that
V_{m+1}
only produces 0- and 1-message states. This completes the proof by induction, and hence we obtain the desired statement.

Finally, note that if
m=M
, then
I(M)=F\not\in\mathcal{K}
for all
\mathcal{K}
, and so the second case in
Eq.

183
never occurs while in the first case the output must be non-vacuum to ensure the overall causal box’s image lies in the 1-message space.
∎

See
2

Proof.

It suffices to show that for every process box protocol
\mathfrak{P}=(\mathcal{C},(\{\mathcal{M}^{A_{k}}_{x_{k}}\}_{x_{k}})_{k=0}^{N+1})
satisfying the properties of
1
there exists a QC-QC protocol
\mathfrak{Q}
which is behaviourally equivalent to
\mathfrak{P}
.

Let
\bar{V}_{1},..,\bar{V}_{M+1}
be a sequence representation of
\mathcal{C}
as in
Lemma

6
.

We note a number of useful facts. Since any restriction of an isometry is again an isometry,

\bar{V}^{\rightarrow I(m)}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{K}\cup I(m)}\bra{\mathcal{K}}+\bar{V}^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{K}}\bra{\mathcal{K}}

(188)

is an isometry for all
m,\mathcal{K}
when restricting the control space to the span of
\ket{\mathcal{K}}
.
For
m=M
, we further have that

\bar{V}^{\rightarrow F}_{\mathcal{K},M+1}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{K}\cup F}\bra{\mathcal{K}}

(189)

is an isometry when restricting the control space to the span of
\ket{\mathcal{K}}
. This immediately implies that
\bar{V}^{\rightarrow F}_{\mathcal{K},M+1}
is also an isometry.

Further, for all
m,p

\bar{V}^{\rightarrow I(m)\dagger}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}\bar{V}^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K},p+1}=0

(190)

as one of the two maps outputs a non-vacuum state while the other outputs a vacuum state on the agent’s wire, which are orthogonal. Similarly, for
m\neq p
we additionally find

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bar{V}^{\rightarrow I(m)\dagger}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}\bar{V}^{\rightarrow I(p)}_{\mathcal{K},p+1}=0\\
\bar{V}^{\rightarrow\Omega\dagger}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}\bar{V}^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K},p+1}=0.\end{aligned}

(191)

This is because one of the two maps outputs on the wire
\alpha_{m+1}
while the other one outputs onto
\alpha_{p+1}
which we can treat as orthogonal.

We then also have that

\bar{V}_{m+1,\mathcal{K}}:=\bar{V}^{\rightarrow I(m)}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}+\bar{V}^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}

(192)

is an isometry as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bar{V}_{m+1,\mathcal{K}}^{\dagger}\bar{V}_{m+1,\mathcal{K}}=&(\bar{V}^{\rightarrow I(m)}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}+\bar{V}^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K},m+1})^{\dagger}(\bar{V}^{\rightarrow I(m)}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}+\bar{V}^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K},m+1})\\
=&\bar{V}^{\rightarrow I(m)\dagger}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}\bar{V}^{\rightarrow I(m)}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}+\bar{V}^{\rightarrow\Omega\dagger}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}\bar{V}^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}\\
=&\bra{\mathcal{K}}(\bar{V}^{\rightarrow I(m)}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{K}\cup I(m)}\bra{\mathcal{K}}+\bar{V}^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{K}}\bra{\mathcal{K}})^{\dagger}\\
&(\bar{V}^{\rightarrow I(m)}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{K}\cup I(m)}\bra{\mathcal{K}}+\bar{V}^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}\otimes\ket{\mathcal{K}}\bra{\mathcal{K}})\ket{\mathcal{K}}\\
=&\mathbb{1}^{A^{I}_{I(m)},t=2m+2}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{\alpha_{m}}.\end{aligned}

(193)

where in the second line we used
Eq.

190
and in the last line we used that
Eq.

188
is an isometry when acting on the control in state
\ket{\mathcal{K}}
.

We define now

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} V^{\rightarrow I(m)}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}(\cdot)&:=\bra{t=2m+2}\bar{V}^{\rightarrow I(m)}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}|_{A^{O,t=2m+1}_{O(m)}\alpha^{\prime}_{m}}(\cdot\otimes\ket{t=2m+1})\\
V^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}(\cdot)&:=\ket{\Omega}^{A^{O}_{O(m+1)}}\bra{\Omega,t=2m+2}^{A^{I}_{I(m)}}\bar{V}^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}|_{A^{O,t=2m+1}_{O(m)}\alpha^{\prime}_{m}}(\cdot\otimes\ket{t=2m+1}).\end{aligned}

(194)

Here, the space
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{m}}
is a finite-dimensional subspace of the in general infinite-dimensional space
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{m}}
. Since
V^{\rightarrow I(0)}_{\emptyset,1}:\mathcal{H}^{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{I(0)}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}},V^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\emptyset,1}:\mathcal{H}^{P}\rightarrow\ket{\Omega}^{A^{I}_{I(0)}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}}
have finite-dimensional domain their images (and thus the span of the union of their images) must be finite-dimensional as well. Hence, there must exist a finite-dimensional subspace
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}}
such that
\text{Im}(V^{\rightarrow I(0)}_{\emptyset,1}),\text{Im}(V^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\emptyset,1})\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{I(0)}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}
and we can write

V^{\rightarrow I(0)}_{\emptyset,1},V^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\emptyset,1}:\mathcal{H}^{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{I(0)}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}.

(195)

We iterate this procedure and find a subspace
\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{m+1}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{m+1}}
for each
m
with
\text{Im}(V^{\rightarrow I(m)}_{\mathcal{K},m+1}),\text{Im}(V^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K},m+1})\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{A^{I}_{I(m)}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{m+1}}
for all
\mathcal{K}
and then restrict the co-domain analogously to
Eq.

195
. The maps defined in
Eq.

194
are hence linear maps with finite-dimensional domain and co-domain, making them appropriate for the QC-QC framework.

We define now the internal operations of the QC-QC, (in the below sum the terms corresponding to
m^{\prime}=m
are
V^{\rightarrow I(m^{\prime})}_{\mathcal{K}_{n},m^{\prime}+1}
)

V^{\rightarrow k_{n+1}}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime},m:m^{\prime}\geq m\\
O(m)=k_{n},I(m^{\prime})=k_{n+1}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{V^{\rightarrow I(m^{\prime})}_{\mathcal{K}_{n},m^{\prime}+1}\circ V^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K}_{n},m^{\prime}}\circ...\circ V^{\rightarrow\Omega}_{\mathcal{K}_{n},m+1}|_{\mathcal{H}^{A^{O}_{O(m)}\alpha^{\prime}_{m}}}}_{=:V^{\rightarrow k_{n+1},m-1\rightarrow m^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{K}_{n-1},k_{n}}}

(196)

where
\mathcal{K}_{n}
is a shorthand for
\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\cup k_{n}
and

V_{n+1}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{K}_{n-1}\\
